Some interactions between Hopf Galois extensions and noncommutative rings Issue: 2 DOI: 10.11144/Javeriana.SC271.sibh
Fabio Calderón, Armando Reyes*

Abstract
In this paper, our objects of interest are Hopf Galois extensions (e.g., Hopf algebras, Galois field extensions, strongly graded algebras, crossed products, principal bundles, etc.) and families of noncommutative rings (e.g., skew polynomial rings, PBW extensions and skew PBW extensions, etc.). We collect and systematize questions, problems, properties and recent advances in both theories by explicitly developing examples and doing calculations that are usually omitted in the literature. In particular, for Hopf Galois extensions we consider approaches from the point of view of quantum torsors (also known as quantum heaps) and Hopf Galois systems, while for some families of noncommutative rings we present advances in the characterization of ring-theoretic and homological properties. Every developed topic is exemplified with abundant references to classic and current works, so this paper serves as a survey for those interested in either of the two theories. Throughout, interactions between both are presented.



Some interactions between Hopf Galois extensions and noncommutative rings

keywords

1
Introduction

In the last half-century, Hopf algebras turned out to be a great tool for studying a large number of problems in several contexts: from providing solutions for the Yang-Baxter equation and describing the so-called quantum groups –appearing in theoretical physics and algebraic theory–, to generalizing Galois theory. It is precisely this last instance what concerns us in this paper.

Classically, Galois theory studies and classifies automorphism groups of fields. In 1965 the theory was generalized to groups acting on commutative rings
[
1
]
, and in 1969 extended to commutative algebras by replacing the action of a group on the algebra for a coaction of a Hopf algebra on a commutative algebra
[
2
]
. The first general definition of
Hopf Galois extensions
is due to Kreimer and Takeuchi
[
3
]
, although the authors restricted their study to the finite-dimensional case. In Part
2
, we will address the modern definition of such extensions, not without first recalling some basic notions regarding the theory of Hopf algebras. Our aim is to provide a large number of examples and properties, developing proofs and calculations that are usually omitted in the literature. We finish the section by giving two recent alternative approaches for Hopf Galois theory:
quantum torsors
(or
quantum heaps
), defined independently by Grunspan
[
4
]
and Škoda
[
5
]
, and
Hopf Galois systems
, introduced by Bichon
[
6
]
. We address the equivalence between these three notions.

Almost in parallel to the first appearance of Hopf algebras, Ore introduced in 1933 a new class of noncommutative rings, nowadays known as
skew polynomial rings
(or
Ore extensions
)
[
7
]
. Although the aim of Ore was to find noncommutative algebras which could be embedded on division rings (e.g.,
[
8
, Chapter 8]
), these structures belong, per se, to a branch of study in algebra used to describe many rings and algebras, mostly coming from mathematical physics and with broad applications in quantum mechanics. Therefore, some classic results such as the Hilbert’s Basis Theorem or the Hilbert’s Syzygy Theorem have been generalized to these objects (see Theorem
3.12
or, e.g.,
[
9
, Sections 2.9 and 3.1]
), while many other properties are still being studied. Hence, at the start of Part
3
we review basic definitions and results on skew polynomial rings, along with some remarkable examples.

However, this is not the only family of noncommutative rings (or algebras) that has been defined since then. Inspired by the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt (PBW) theorem for enveloping algebras of Lie algebras, Bell and Goodearl defined in 1988 the
PBW extensions

[
10
]
. These consist of polynomial-type rings having a PBW basis and specific commutation rules. Furthermore, Gallego and Lezama in 2011 generalized the notion to
skew PBW extensions
so new examples of rings with polynomial behavior could be studied
[
11
,
12
]
. Hence, our aim is to also address these types of rings and some examples of the theory. We also study a quite different collection of algebras, known as
almost symmetric algebras
(or
Sridharan enveloping algebras
). These generalize enveloping algebras via twisting by
2
-cocycles, without losing some nice properties
[
13
]
.

With these two overviews in mind, one could ask for possible relations between some of the mentioned families and Hopf algebras (e.g.
[
14
,
15
]
), and in particular, with Hopf Galois theory. Therefore, as an original contribution, we study coactions of arbitrary Hopf algebras over skew polynomial rings. Also, following
[
16
]
, we attach a Hopf Galois system to almost symmetric algebras, and elucidate the structure of quantum torsor present in Kashiwara algebras.

Therefore, the purpose of this paper is three-fold: as a survey of classic and current works on Hopf Galois theory, as a quick overview of several approaches to noncommutative algebras appearing in applications, and as a compilation (with some original developments) of the interactions between both theories. This work is the result of the first author’s master’s thesis, which had a Meritorious Mention at Universidad Nacional de Colombia and was written under the advice of the second author.

Notations and conventions

Throughout this manuscript all rings and their morphisms are unitary.
K
will denote an arbitrary commutative ring and a field (if necessary, algebraically closed and of characteristic 0). Unless stated otherwise, tensor products are assumed to be over
K
and every
K
-module is non-zero.

Let
f,g,h
be functions. We denote the composition of
f
with
g
by
fg
and the composition of
h
with itself
n
-times as
h^{n}
. The arrow
\operatorname{id}_{X}:X\rightarrow X
will always denote the identity map of
X
.

Arrow diagrams
will be constantly used, representing composition of functions as concatenation of arrows. A diagram is said to be
commutative
if, no matter what path one follows, the composition of arrows (functions) returns always the same result.

The symbols
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
denote the usual numerical systems, assuming that
0\in\mathbb{N}
.

2
Hopf Galois theory: preliminaries, definitions and examples

Although we assume some familiarity with the theory of Hopf algebras, for the purpose of a self-contained document, this section will start addressing basic terminology of (co)algebras (Section
2.1
and
2.2
), some examples (Section
2.3
), and (co)modules, Hopf modules and (co)actions (Sections
2.4
to
2.5
). Then we introduce in Section
2.6
the concept of Hopf Galois extension, which is transversal to this work. A large amount of examples and properties are presented in Sections
2.7
and
2.8
. Finally, and following recent developments, we dedicate Sections
2.9
and
2.10
to two alternative –and, under some conditions, equivalent– approaches of Hopf Galois extensions, namely quantum torsors and Hopf Galois systems.

Except for the last two sections, all definitions and results presented in this part are classical and can be consulted, for example, in
[
2
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
3
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
17
]
.

2.1
Algebras and coalgebras

Recall that a
K
-algebra
is a
K
-module
A
together with two
K
-linear maps,
m:A\otimes A\rightarrow A
and
u:K\rightarrow A
, such that the following diagrams are commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The first diagram is known as the
associativity property
while the second as the
main unit property
. We write
ab=m(a\otimes b)
and
1_{A}:=u(1_{K})
. Similarly, a
K
-coalgebra
is a
K
-module
C
together with two
K
-linear maps,
\Delta:C\rightarrow C\otimes C
and
\varepsilon:C\rightarrow K
, such that the following diagrams are commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes C\otimes C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{C}\otimes\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{C}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{C}\otimes\varepsilon}

The map
\Delta
is called the
comultiplication
and
\varepsilon
the
counit
. The left diagram is known as the
coassociativity property
while the second as the
main counit property
. We use the widely accepted
Heyneman–Sweedler notation
for the comultiplication, that is, for any
c\in C
we write
\Delta(c)=c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}\in C\otimes C
.

Arrows between these structures are defined as those preserving the operations. Therefore we consider
K
-
\operatorname{Alg}
, the category of
K
-algebras, and
K
-
\operatorname{Cog}
, the category of
K
-coalgebras. Regarding substructures in
K
-
\operatorname{Cog}
, recall that for a coalgebra
C
, a submodule
D
is called a
subcoalgebra
if
\Delta(D)\subseteq D\otimes D
, and a submodule
I
is called a
left
(resp.
right
)
coideal
if
\Delta(I)\subseteq C\otimes I
(resp.
\Delta(I)\subseteq I\otimes C
). Also, a submodule
J
of
C
is called a
coideal
if
\Delta(J)\subseteq J\otimes C+C\otimes J
and
\varepsilon(J)=0
.

Given two
K
-modules
M,N
, the
twist map

\tau_{M,N}:M\otimes N\rightarrow N\otimes M
is defined by
m\otimes n\mapsto n\otimes m
, for all
m\in M
and
n\in N
. Sometimes this arrow is also denoted by
\tau_{(12)}
when emphasis in the interchanged coordinates is needed. In the situation
M=N
, we simply write
\tau_{M}
. An algebra
A
is
commutative
if
m\tau_{A}=m
, while a coalgebra
C
is
cocommutative
if
\tau_{C}\Delta=\Delta
, that is, if
c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}=c_{(2)}\otimes c_{(1)}
, for all
c\in C
.

If
M
is a
K
-module, for
n\geq 2
, we denote
M^{\otimes n}:=M\otimes\dotsb\otimes M
(
n
times). By definition,
M^{\otimes 0}:=K
and
M^{\otimes 1}:=M
. This notation is useful when dealing with coalgebras and their maps. For any coalgebra
C
, we define the sequence of maps
\{\Delta_{n}:C\rightarrow C^{\otimes(n+1)}\}_{n\geq 1}
recurrently as follows:
\Delta_{1}:=\Delta
, and
\Delta_{n}:=(\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{C}^{n-1})\Delta_{n-1}
, for any
n\geq 2
.

The coassociativity of
\Delta
states that, for any
c\in C
,

({c_{(1)}}_{(1)}\otimes{c_{(1)}}_{(2)})\otimes c_{(2)}=c_{(1)}\otimes({c_{(2)}}_{(1)}\otimes{c_{(2)}}_{(2)}),

and therefore we are able to just write
\Delta_{2}(c)=c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}\otimes c_{(3)}
. Moreover, we will have

\Delta_{n}(c)=c_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{(n+1)},
for any
n\geq 1.

The main property of the counit
\varepsilon
may be formulated as
\varepsilon(c_{(1)})c_{(2)}=c=c_{(1)}\varepsilon(c_{(2)})
. Also, the behavior of a coalgebra morphism
g:C\rightarrow D
can be stated as
g(c)_{(1)}\otimes g(c)_{(2)}=g(c_{(1)})\otimes g(c_{(2)})
.

2.2
Bialgebras and Hopf algebras

Recall that a
K
-bialgebra

H
is a
K
-module simultaneously endowed with an algebra and a coalgebra structure, both over
K
, satisfying that
m
and
u
are morphisms of coalgebras (or equivalently, that
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are algebra maps). For simplicity, and since it holds for most applications, throughout we will assume the hypothesis that every
K
-bialgebra
H
is flat over
K
, meaning that the tensor product functor
-\otimes H
is exact (i.e., preserves the exactness of sequences). The category of
K
-bialgebras is denoted by
K
-
\operatorname{Bialg}
.

In the
K
-module
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
of all
K
-linear maps between a coalgebra
C
and an algebra
A
we define the
convolution product
as
(f*g)(c):=(m_{A}(f\otimes g)\Delta_{C})(c)
, for all
f,g\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
, and
c\in C
. In Heyneman–Sweedler notation,
(f*g)(c)=f(c_{(1)})g(c_{(2)})
. Hence the convolution product endows
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
with an algebra structure where the identity element is
u_{A}\varepsilon_{C}
. We say that
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
is
convolution invertible
if there exists an element
g\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
such that
f*g=g*f=u_{A}\varepsilon_{C}
.

Definition 2.1

(Hopf algebra)
.

A
K
-bialgebra
H
is a
K
-Hopf algebra
if
\operatorname{id}_{H}
is convolution invertible by an element
S\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(H,H)
. In this case,
S
is called an
antipode
for
H
.

Since
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(H,H)
is an algebra and
S
is defined as an inverse, the antipode is unique. Moreover, using Heyneman–Sweedler notation,
S
satisfies
S(h_{(1)})h_{(2)}=\varepsilon(h)1=h_{(1)}S(h_{(2)})
, for all
h\in H
. This is known as the
main property of the antipode
. It can be shown that
S
is an anti-morphism of algebras and coalgebras (e.g.
[
18
, Proposition 4.2.6]
).

As with previous structures, arrows can be considered between Hopf algebras and therefore one can define the corresponding category, denoted by
K
-
\operatorname{HopfAlg}
. It can be shown that any bialgebra morphism between two Hopf algebras is always a Hopf algebra morphism (e.g.
[
18
, Proposition 4.2.5]
), which in terms of categories means that
K
-
\operatorname{HopfAlg}
is a full subcategory of
K
-
\operatorname{Bialg}
. Regarding substructures in
K
-
\operatorname{HopfAlg}
, for a Hopf algebra
H
, a submodule
L
is said to be a
Hopf subalgebra
if it is a subalgebra of
H
, a subcoalgebra of
H
and
S(L)\subseteq L
. Also, a submodule
I
of
H
is said to be a
Hopf ideal
if it is an ideal of
H
(as algebra), a coideal of
H
(as coalgebra) and
S(I)\subseteq I
.

Finally, recall that in a coalgebra
C
, an element
c\in C
is said to be
group-like
if
\Delta(c)=c\otimes c
. The set of all group-like elements of
C
is denoted by
G(C)
. Similarly,
x\in C
is said to be a
(g,h)
-primitive element
(or simply a
skew primitive element
when
g
and
h
are not specified), if
\Delta(x)=x\otimes g+h\otimes x
with
g,h\in G(C)
. The set of all
(g,h)
-primitive elements of
C
is denoted by
P_{g,h}(C)
. Notice that if
H
is a Hopf algebra,
G(H)
becomes a group with induced multiplication and inverses given by the antipode
S
.

2.3
Examples of Hopf algebras

Now we address some essential examples of Hopf algebras (for a larger amount see e.g.
[
18
,
20
,
21
,
17
]
).

Example 2.2
.

Any commutative ring
K
has structure of
K
-Hopf algebra by defining, for all
k\in K
,

\Delta(k):=k\otimes 1,\quad\varepsilon(k):=k,\quad\quad S(k):=k.

In particular, this holds if
K=
is a field.

The next example establishes a methodical way of constructing new Hopf algebras.

Example 2.3
.

Let
A,B
be two
K
-algebras. Then the
K
-module
A\otimes B
has also the structure of a
K
-algebra with multiplication
m_{A\otimes B}
and unit
u_{A\otimes B}
given by the compositions

Notice that the multiplication can be stated as
(a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime}):=aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}
, for all
a,a^{\prime}\in A
,
b,b^{\prime}\in B
. The unit element is
1_{A}\otimes 1_{B}
. Further results on this algebra can be found in
[
20
, Section II. 4]
. When
A=B
, the multiplication simplifies to
m_{A\otimes A}:=m_{A}\otimes m_{A}
.

Similarly, if
C,D
are two
K
-coalgebras, then
C\otimes D
has also the structure of a
K
-coalgebra with comultiplication
\Delta_{C\otimes D}
and counit
\varepsilon_{C\otimes D}
given by the compositions

In Heyneman–Sweedler notation,

\displaystyle\Delta_{C\otimes D}(c\otimes d)=(c\otimes d)_{(1)}\otimes(c\otimes d)_{(2)}=(c_{(1)}\otimes d_{(1)})\otimes(c_{(2)}\otimes d_{(2)}),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{C\otimes D}(c\otimes d)=\varepsilon_{C}(c)\varepsilon_{D}(d).

When
C=D
, the comultiplication simplifies to
\Delta_{C\otimes C}:=\Delta_{C}\otimes\Delta_{C}
.

Furthermore, if
H,L
are two
K
-Hopf algebras, then their tensor product is also a
K
-Hopf algebra with antipode
S_{H\otimes L}:=S_{H}\otimes S_{L}
.

Example 2.4
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra and
I
a Hopf ideal of
H
. Since
I
is a two-sided ideal of
H
, the quotient module
H/I
already has algebra structure, by putting

\overline{h}\overline{g}:=\overline{hg}
, for all
h,g\in H
, where
\overline{h}:=h+I
. The identity element is
\overline{1}
. Moreover,
H/I
has Hopf algebra structure given by

\overline{\Delta}(\overline{h}):=\overline{h_{(1)}}\otimes\overline{h_{(2)}},\qquad\overline{\varepsilon}(\overline{h}):=\varepsilon(h)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\overline{S}(\overline{h}):=\overline{S(h)},\qquad\mbox{for all }h\in H.

One can easily check that the canonical projection
H\rightarrow H/I
is a surjective morphism of Hopf algebras.

In the following examples, the base ring is a field.

Example 2.5
.

Recall that for any -vector space
V
we denote by
V^{*}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{(}V,)
its
(linear) dual
, consisting of all -linear maps from
V
to , together with the point-wise addition and scalar multiplication by constants so that
V^{*}
is also a -vector space. In particular, we have
{}^{*}\cong
via the identification
f\mapsto f(1_{)}
.

Let
H
be a finite dimensional -Hopf algebra (i.e.,
H
is finite dimensional as -vector space). Then its linear dual
H^{*}
is also a -Hopf algebra with operations

In this example the condition of
H
being finite dimensional cannot be easily dropped, for if
H
is not finite dimensional,
H^{*}\otimes H^{*}
could be a proper subspace of
(H\otimes H)^{*}
and thus the image of
m^{\circ}:H^{*}\rightarrow(H\otimes H)^{*}
might not lie in
H^{*}\otimes H^{*}
. Therefore, for the general case a certain subset
H^{\circ}
of
H^{*}
is considered, often called the
finite dual
of
H
(see e.g.
[
21
, Section 1.2]
). On the other hand, duals for Hopf algebras defined over commutative rings constitute an open line of investigation, and some progress has been made when the base ring is a polynomial algebra (see e.g.
[
25
]
).

Example 2.6

(Group algebra)
.

Let
G
be a (multiplicative) group. The
group algebra
, denoted by
G
, is the -vector space with
G
as a basis, and hence its elements are of the form
\sum_{g\in G}k_{g}g
, where only finite
k_{g}
are non-zero scalars.
G
is an algebra with multiplication given by

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}g_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}l_{j}h_{j}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(k_{i}l_{j})(g_{i}h_{j}),\qquad\mbox{for all }k_{i},l_{j}\in,\,g_{i},h_{j}\in G,

and unit
1_{G}:=1_{G}
. Furthermore,
G
becomes a Hopf algebra by linearly extending the following rules:

\Delta(g):=g\otimes g,\qquad\varepsilon(g):=1\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S(g):=g^{-1},\qquad\mbox{for all }g\in G.

Example 2.7

(Dual of group algebra)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
H=(G)^{*}
the dual Hopf algebra of the group algebra. Even though Example
2.5
describes the operations of
H
, we want a more detailed description. So notice that the universal property of
G
allows us to identify
H
with
G
, the algebra of functions from
G
to . Hence we have for
f,g\in^{G}
and
x,y\in G
:

\displaystyle(f\cdot g)(x)

\displaystyle:=[m^{\circ}(f\otimes g)](x)=[\Delta^{*}(f\otimes g)](x)

\displaystyle=[(f\otimes g)\Delta](x)=(f\otimes g)(x\otimes x)=f(x)g(x),

\displaystyle[\Delta(f)](x\otimes y)

\displaystyle:=[\Delta^{\circ}(f)](x\otimes y)=[m^{*}(f)](x\otimes y)=f(xy),

\displaystyle[S(f)](x)

\displaystyle:=[S^{\circ}(f)](x)=[S^{*}(f)](x)=[fS](x)=f(S(x)).

Despite these formulas, a full description of
\Delta(f)
is not given. Therefore we define for every
x\in G
the map
p_{x}:G\rightarrow
given by

p_{x}(y):=\delta_{x,y}:=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if } \mbox{$x=y$},\\
0&\mbox{if } \mbox{$x\neq y$}.\end{cases}

Since
G
is finite,
\{p_{x}:x\in G\}
is a basis for
G
(in correspondence with the dual basis of
(G)^{*}
). For those elements, we have

\Delta(p_{x})=\sum_{uv=x}p_{u}\otimes p_{v}=\sum_{y\in G}p_{y}\otimes p_{y^{-1}x},

which describes the comultiplication for basis elements.

Example 2.8

(Tensor algebra)
.

Let
V
be a -vector space. An algebra
T(V)
is said to be a
tensor algebra of
V
if there exists a linear map
\iota:V\rightarrow T(V)
such that the following universal property is satisfied: for any algebra
A
and any linear map
f:V\rightarrow A
there exists an unique algebra morphism
\overline{f}:T(V)\rightarrow A
such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

The tensor algebra
T(V)
is unique up to isomorphism and can be described as
T(V)=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}V^{\otimes i}
, meaning that any element of
T(V)
has the form
z=(z_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, where
z_{i}\in V^{\otimes i}
and almost all
z_{i}
vanish. The multiplication is given by the rule

(v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i})(v_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\otimes v_{i+j})=v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{i+j},\qquad\mbox{for all }i,j\geq 0.

The identity element is
1\in V^{\otimes 0}=
.
T(V)
becomes a Hopf algebra by extending (via the universal property) the rules

\Delta(v):=v\otimes 1+1\otimes v,\qquad\varepsilon(v):=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S(v):=-v,\qquad\mbox{for all }v\in V.

A complete proof of this fact can be found in
[
18
, Section 4.3.2]
.

Example 2.9

(Symmetric algebra)
.

Let
V
be a -vector space. A commutative algebra
S(V)
is said to be a
symmetric algebra of
V
if there exists a linear map
l:V\rightarrow S(V)
such that the following universal property is satisfied: for any commutative algebra
A
and any linear map
h:V\rightarrow A
there exists an unique algebra morphism
\overline{h}:S(V)\rightarrow A
such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}

A straightforward use of the universal property shows that
S(V)
is unique up to isomorphism. The existence of symmetric algebras is shown by explicitly construction of
S(V)
as the quotient
T(V)/I
, where
I=\langle u\otimes v-v\otimes u:u,v\in V\rangle

[
18
, Section 4.3.3]
. An alternative construction can be found in
[
9
, Section 15.1.18]
. Since for all
u,v\in V
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{T(V)}(u\otimes v-v\otimes u)

\displaystyle=\Delta(u)\Delta(v)-\Delta(v)\Delta(u)

\displaystyle=(u\otimes 1+1\otimes u)(v\otimes 1+1\otimes v)-(v\otimes 1+1\otimes v)(u\otimes 1-1\otimes u)

\displaystyle=(u\otimes v-v\otimes u)\otimes 1+1\otimes(u\otimes v-v\otimes u),

which is an element of
I\otimes T(V)+T(V)\otimes I
, and

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{T(V)}(u\otimes v-v\otimes u)=\varepsilon(u)\varepsilon(v)-\varepsilon(v)\varepsilon(u)=0,

\displaystyle S_{T(V)}(u\otimes v-v\otimes u)=S(u)S(v)-S(v)S(u)=(-y)\otimes(-x)-(-x)\otimes(-y)\in I,

we have shown that
I
is a Hopf ideal of
T(V)
. Hence, by Example
2.4
,
S(V)=T(V)/I
is a (commutative) Hopf algebra with induced operations.

Example 2.10

(Universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra)
.

A -vector space
\mathfrak{g}
is a
Lie algebra
if there exists a bilinear map
[-,-]:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
, called the
Lie bracket
, such that the following conditions hold:

(L1)

(
Antisymmetry
)
[x,y]=-[y,x]
, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
,

(L2)

(
Jacobi identity
)
[[x,y],z]+[[z,x],y]+[[y,z],x]=0
, for all
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g}
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is called
Abelian
if
[x,y]=0
for every
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
. In general the Lie bracket is not associative. Moreover,
(L1)
implies
[x,x]=0
, for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
. For example,
\mathbb{R}^{3}
equipped with the usual vector product is a
\mathbb{R}
-Lie algebra.

Any (associative) algebra
A
can be endowed with a -Lie algebra structure with
[a,b]:=ab-ba
, for all
a,b\in A
. In this example we shall consider the converse construction, i.e., an associative algebra rising from a given Lie algebra. The importance of this construction is well known, going from representation theory (e.g.
[
26
]
), construction of Verma modules (e.g.
[
27
, Section 9.5]
) or characterization of left-invariant differential operators (e.g.
[
28
, Chapter II]
), to cocommutative cases of quantum groups (e.g.
[
20
]
).

If
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
are two Lie algebras, a linear map
f:\mathfrak{g}_{1}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is an
morphism of Lie algebras
if
f([x,y])=[f(x),f(y)]
, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
. In particular, if
\mathfrak{g}_{2}=A
is an associative algebra endowed with the Lie bracket mentioned above, we say that the map
f:\mathfrak{g}_{1}\rightarrow A
is a
representation
(of
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
).

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra. An associative algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
is an
universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, if there exists a representation
f:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow U(\mathfrak{g})
such that the following universal property is satisfied: for any associative algebra
A
and any representation
h:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow A
there exists an unique algebra morphism
\overline{h}:U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow A
such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}

It follows that
\overline{h}
is also a Lie algebra map and that
U(\mathfrak{g})
is unique up to isomorphism. The existence of such enveloping algebra is given by explicitly constructing
U(\mathfrak{g})
as the quotient algebra
\langle X\rangle/I
, where
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
,
\langle X\rangle
is the free -algebra over
X
and
I=\langle x_{i}x_{j}-x_{j}x_{i}-[x_{i},x_{j}]:x_{j},x_{i}\in X\rangle

[
20
, Section V.2]
. An alternative construction
[
18
, Section 4.3.4]
is done by taking
U(\mathfrak{g})
as the quotient
T(\mathfrak{g})/J
, where
T(\mathfrak{g})
is the tensor algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
and

J=\langle[x,y]-x\otimes y+y\otimes x:x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\rangle.

(1)

In either case, the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt Theorem establishes that if there exists a total order
\preceq
in
X
, then the set containing 1 and all elements of the form
x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{n}}
, with
x_{i_{1}}\preceq\cdots\preceq x_{i_{n}}
, constitutes a -basis of
U(\mathfrak{g})
(e.g.
[
29
, Theorem V.3]
). The rules

\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x,\quad\varepsilon(x)=0,\quad\varepsilon(x)=-x,\quad\mbox{for all }x\in\mathfrak{g},

(2)

can be extended to
U(\mathfrak{g})
by applying the universal property or, alternatively, verifying that
J
as in (
1
) is a Hopf ideal. Either way, (
2
) makes
U(\mathfrak{g})
a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

Example 2.11

(Universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}()
)
.

Denote by
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}()
the -algebra consisting of all
n\times n
matrices with entries in seen as a Lie algebra. One can easily check that the elements

x=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}0&1\\
0&0\end{array}\right),\qquad y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}0&0\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\qquad h=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\qquad i=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),

form a basis for
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}()
. Moreover,
[x,y]=h
,
[h,x]=2x
,
[h,y]=-2y
, and
[i,x]=[i,y]=[i,h]=0
. We denote by
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}()
the subspace of all matrices with null trace. A basis is
\{x,y,h\}
. For the particular case
=\mathbb{C}
a detailed study of this Lie algebra can be found in
[
20
, Chapter V]
. By the previous example,
U(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}())
can be seen as the Hopf algebra generated by
x,y,h
subject to the relation
[x,y]=h
,
[h,x]=2x
and
[h,y]=-2y
. In Part
3
, we will endow this algebra with another structure, evidencing that a single object can be enriched with several distinct structures.

For the next example, recall that
\omega\in
is said to be a
n
-th root of unity
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
) if
\omega^{n}=1
. Furthermore,
\omega
is
primitive
if it is not a
k
-th root of unity for some
k<n
.

Example 2.12

(Taft Hopf algebra)
.

Given a
n
-th root of unity
\omega
in , the
n^{2}
-dimensional
Taft Hopf algebra
is given as an algebra by
T_{n^{2}}(\omega)=\langle g,x\rangle/\langle g^{n}-1,x^{n},xg-\omega gx\rangle
.
T_{n^{2}}(\omega)
acquires structure of non-(co)commutative Hopf algebra via

\displaystyle\Delta(g)=g\otimes g,\qquad\varepsilon(g)=1,\qquad S(g)=g^{-1},

\displaystyle\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+g\otimes x,\qquad\varepsilon(x)=0,\qquad S(x)=-g^{-1}x.

Since the construction depends on the choice of
\omega
, there are
\Phi(n)
non-isomorphic Taft Hopf algebras for each dimension
n^{2}
, where
\Phi
denotes Euler’s totient function. These Hopf algebras were constructed as examples of finite dimensional Hopf algebras having antipodes of arbitrarily high order, since in
T_{n^{2}}(\omega)
,
S
has order
2n

[
30
]
. The case
n=2
is also known as the
Sweedler Hopf algebra
.

Example 2.13

(Quantum enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}()
)
.

Let
q\in
be an invertible element such that
q\neq\pm 1
. We define
U_{q}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}())
as the algebra generated by
e,f,k,k^{-1}
subject to the relations

\displaystyle kk^{-1}=k^{-1}k=1,\qquad kek^{-1}=q^{2}e,\qquad kfk^{-1}=q^{-2}f,

\displaystyle\left[e,f\right]=ef-fe=\frac{k-k^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}.

It can be shown that
\{e^{i}f^{j}k^{l}:i,j\in\mathbb{N},\,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is a basis
[
20
, Proposition VII.1.1]
. For simplicity, set
=\mathbb{C}
and
q\in\mathbb{C}
not being a root of unity. Hence
U_{q}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-Hopf algebra with the operations induced by

\displaystyle\Delta(e):=1\otimes e+e\otimes k,\qquad\Delta(f):=k^{-1}\otimes f+f\otimes 1,\qquad\Delta(k):=k\otimes k,

\displaystyle\Delta(k^{-1}):=k^{-1}\otimes k^{-1},\qquad\varepsilon(e)=\varepsilon(f)=0,\qquad\varepsilon(k)=\varepsilon(k^{-1})=1,

\displaystyle S(e):=-ek^{-1},\qquad S(f):=-kf,\qquad S(k):=k^{-1},\qquad S(k^{-1}):=k.

Moreover, if
q^{2}
is a
n
-th primitive root of unity, the elements
k^{n}-1
,
e^{n}
and
f^{n}
are skew-primitive. Hence the ideal generated by them is a Hopf ideal
[
31
, Proposition 1.7]
and thus
U_{q}^{\prime}:=U_{q}/\langle k^{n}-1,e^{n},f^{n}\rangle
is a Hopf algebra, known as the
Frobenius-Lusztig kernel
.

Example 2.14

(Circle Hopf algebra)
.

Let
H
be the algebra defined as
H_{:}=\langle c,s\rangle/I
, with
I=\langle c^{2}+s^{2}-1,cs\rangle
. Then
H
is a Hopf algebra via

\displaystyle\Delta(c)=c\otimes c-s\otimes s,\qquad\varepsilon(c)=1,\qquad S(c)=c,

\displaystyle\Delta(s)=c\otimes s+s\otimes c,\qquad\varepsilon(s)=0,\qquad S(s)=-s.

As we shall see in Example
2.7.3
, this algebra naturally appears in some examples of separable field extensions not being Galois, but still satisfying the defining condition of a Hopf Galois extension.

2.4
Modules and comodules

The aim of this section is to introduce (co)actions of Hopf algebras over arbitrary
K
-algebras; these are of utmost importance for Hopf Galois extensions. Therefore, we review the notions of (co)module over an algebra and (co)module algebra. Throughout this section
A
will denote an arbitrary
K
-algebra, while
C
a
K
-coalgebra.

Recall that a
left
A
-module
is a
K
-module
M
together with a
K
-linear map
\gamma:A\otimes M\rightarrow M
, called the
scalar product map
of
M
, such that
\gamma(m\otimes\operatorname{id}_{M})=\gamma(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\gamma)
and
\gamma(u\otimes\operatorname{id}_{M})(k\otimes x)=kx
, for all
k\in K
and
x\in M
. We write
a\cdot x:=\gamma(a\otimes x)
, so the above means that
(ab)\cdot x=a\cdot(b\cdot x)
and
1\cdot x=x
, for all
x\in M
and
a,b\in A
. Right modules over
A
are defined similarly, the difference being that the scalar product map has the form
\gamma:M\otimes A\rightarrow M
.

Similarly, a
right
C
-comodule
is a
K
-module
N
together with a
K
-linear map
\rho:N\rightarrow N\otimes C
, called the
structure map
of
N
, such that
(\operatorname{id}_{N}\otimes\Delta)\rho=(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{C})\rho
and
(\operatorname{id}_{N}\otimes\varepsilon)\rho(n)=n\otimes 1
, for all
n\in N
. Left comodules over
C
are defined in the same way, having structure map of the form
\rho:N\rightarrow C\otimes N
.

We extend Heyneman–Sweedler notation to comodules. Let
N
be a right
C
-comodule with structure map
\rho:N\rightarrow N\otimes C
. For any
n\in N
, the element
\rho(n)
of
N\otimes C
shall be written as
\rho(n)=n_{(0)}\otimes n_{(1)}
, fixing the convention that
n_{(j)}\in C
for
j\neq 0
. With this, the defining properties of a right comodule may be written as

\displaystyle(n_{(0)})_{(0)}\otimes(n_{(0)})_{(1)}\otimes n_{(1)}=n_{(0)}\otimes(n_{(1)})_{(1)}\otimes(n_{(1)})_{(2)}=n_{(0)}\otimes n_{(1)}\otimes n_{(2)},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(n_{(1)})n_{(0)}=n.

(3)

Likewise, if
N
is a left
C
-comodule with structure map
\rho:N\rightarrow C\otimes N
, preserving the convention for non-zero indexes, we write
\rho(n)=n_{(-1)}\otimes n_{(0)}
.

Given two right
C
-comodules
N
and
L
, with structure maps
\rho_{N}
and
\rho_{L}
, respectively, a
K
-linear map
g:N\rightarrow Y
is a
comodule morphism
if
g(n)_{(0)}\otimes g(n)_{(1)}=g(n_{(0)})\otimes n_{(1)}
, for all
n\in N
. We denote the category of left (resp. right)
A
-modules by
{}_{A}{\operatorname{Mod}}
(resp.
\operatorname{Mod}_{A}
). Similarly, the category of right (resp. left) comodules over a coalgebra
C
is denoted by
\operatorname{Mod}^{C}
(resp.
{}^{C}{\operatorname{Mod}}
).

The definition of a bimodule over an algebra can also be dualized. Indeed, given two coalgebras
C,D
, a
K
-module
N
is said to be a
(D,C)
-bicomodule
if
N
is a left
D
-comodule with structure map
\mu:N\rightarrow D\otimes N
,
N
is a right
C
-comodule with structure map
\rho:N\rightarrow N\otimes C
, and
(\mu\otimes\operatorname{id}_{C})\rho=(\operatorname{id}_{D}\otimes\rho)\mu
.
The later condition may be written in Heyneman–Sweedler notation as

(n_{(0)})_{{(-1)}}\otimes(n_{(0)})_{(0)}\otimes n_{(1)}=n_{{(-1)}}\otimes(n_{(0)})_{(0)}\otimes(n_{(0)})_{(1)},\qquad\mbox{for all }n\in N.

A
morphism of bicomodules
is a linear map between two
(D,C)
-bicomodules which is both a morphism of left
D
-comodules and a morphism of right
C
-modules. Hence, we can define the correspondent category, which is denoted by
{}^{D}{\operatorname{Mod}}^{C}
. Similarly, given two algebras
A,B
, the category of
(B,A)
-bimodules is denoted by
{}_{B}{\operatorname{Mod}}_{A}
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra. For a left
H
-module
M
,
the set of invariants of
H
on
M
is

M^{H}:=\{m\in M:h\cdot m=\varepsilon(h)m,\ \forall h\in H\}.

Similarly, for a right
H
-comodule
N
with structure map
\rho:N\rightarrow N\otimes H
,
the set of coinvariants of
H
on
N
is given by

N^{\operatorname{co}H}:=\{n\in N:\rho(n)=n\otimes 1\}.

When the base ring is a field a natural question is whether there exists a relation between the comodules of
H
and the modules of the dual Hopf algebra
H^{*}
. The following result shows that, at least in the finite-dimensional case, there is such a correspondence preserving (co)invariants.

Proposition 2.15

(e.g.
[
21
, Lemma 1.7.2]
)
.

Let
H
be a finite-dimensional -Hopf algebra and
H^{*}
its dual Hopf algebra. Then, for any -vector space
N
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

N
is a right
H
-comodule.

(ii)

N
is a left
H^{*}
-module.

Moreover, under these conditions,
N^{H^{*}}=N^{\operatorname{co}H}
.

Proof 2.16
.

Let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
be a basis for
H
and
\{e_{1}^{*},\ldots,e_{n}^{*}\}
the corresponding dual basis for
H^{*}
(i.e.,
e_{i}^{*}(e_{j})=\delta_{ij}
, the Kronecker delta). If
N
is a right
H
-comodule, then
N
becomes a
H^{*}
-module via

f\cdot n:=f(n_{(1)})n_{(0)},\qquad\mbox{for all }f\in H^{*},\,n\in N.

(4)

Reciprocally, if
N
is a left
H^{*}
-module, then
N
becomes a right
H
-comodule with structure map
\rho:N\rightarrow N\otimes H
via

\rho(a):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{*}\cdot a\otimes e_{i}.

(5)

We omit the details in these two implications since these are a straightforward verification of the defining conditions. Finally, using the notation of Example
2.5
, we have

\displaystyle N^{H^{*}}

\displaystyle=\{n\in N:f\cdot a=u^{\circ}(f)n,\,\forall f\in H^{*}\}

\displaystyle=\{n\in N:f(n_{(1)})n_{(0)}=(fu)(1_{)}n,\forall f\in H^{*}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{n\in N:n_{(0)}f(n_{(1)})=f(1_{H})n,\forall f\in H^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{n\in N:(\operatorname{id}_{N}\otimes f)(\rho(n))=(\operatorname{id}_{N}\otimes f)(n\otimes 1),\forall f\in H^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle=\{n\in N:\rho(n)=n\otimes 1\}=N^{\operatorname{co}H},

which shows the desired equality.

Now, we review some essential examples of modules and comodules.

Example 2.17
.

Any
K
-algebra
A
is a left module over itself by taking
\gamma=m_{A}
(in other words,
a\cdot b=ab
, for all
a,b\in A
). Similarly, any
K
-coalgebra
C
is a right comodule over itself by taking
\rho=\Delta_{C}
.

Example 2.18
.

Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra and let
V,W
be two left
H
-modules. Then
V\otimes W
has also structure of left
H
-module via

h\cdot(v\otimes w):=(h_{(1)}\cdot v)\otimes(h_{(2)}\cdot w),\qquad\mbox{for all }h\in H,v\in V,w\in W.

If
\gamma_{V}
and
\gamma_{W}
are the respective structure maps of
V
and
W
, the above means that the structure map
\gamma_{V\otimes W}
is defined as the composition

\gamma_{V\otimes W}:=(\gamma_{V}\otimes\gamma_{W})(\operatorname{id}_{H}\otimes\tau_{H,V}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W})(\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W}).

Example 2.19
.

Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra and let
V,W
two right
H
-comodules with respective structure maps
\rho_{V}
and
\rho_{W}
. Then
V\otimes W
is also a right
H
-comodule by taking
\rho_{V\otimes W}
as the composition

\rho_{V\otimes W}=(\operatorname{id}_{V}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W}\otimes m)(\operatorname{id}_{V}\otimes\tau_{H,W}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})(\rho_{V}\otimes\rho_{W}),

i.e.,
\rho_{V\otimes W}(v\otimes w)=v_{(0)}\otimes w_{(0)}\otimes v_{(1)}w_{(1)}
, for all
v\in V
and
w\in W
.

The previous examples implicitly describe the structure of monoidal category that both
{}_{H}{\operatorname{Mod}}
and
\operatorname{Mod}^{H}
possess (see e.g.
[
21
, Section 10.4]
for the definition).

We end this section by discussing Hopf modules. Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra. Then, similarly to
H
being both an algebra and a coalgebra with certain compatibility, a Hopf module over
H
will be both an
H
-module and an
H
-comodule in which the structure map is a module map. Namely, a
K
-module
M
is a
right-right
H
-Hopf module
if
M
is a right
H
-module,
M
is a right
H
-comodule with structure map
\rho:M\rightarrow M\otimes H
, and
\rho(m\cdot h)=(m\cdot h)_{(0)}\otimes(m\cdot h)_{(1)}=m_{(0)}\cdot h_{(1)}\otimes m_{(1)}h_{(2)}
, for all
m\in M
and
h\in H
.

In the first defining condition,
H
may be replaced by a Hopf subalgebra
L
of
H
. In this case we say that
M
is a
right-right
(H,L)
-Hopf module
. The category of all right-right
(H,L)
-Hopf modules is denoted by
\operatorname{Mod}_{L}^{H}
, in which morphisms are
K
-linear maps also being both morphisms of right
L
-modules and morphisms of right
H
-comodules. Clearly, by changing laterality and modifying the compatibility condition, we also obtain the categories
{}^{H}{\operatorname{Mod}}_{L}
,
{}_{L}{\operatorname{Mod}}^{H}
and
{}^{H}_{L}{\operatorname{Mod}}
.

Example 2.20
.

Any
K
-Hopf algebra
H
is a
H
-Hopf module via
\rho=\Delta
.

Example 2.21

(Trivial Hopf module)
.

Let
M
be any right
H
-module. Then
M\otimes H
is a right-right
H
-Hopf module using
\rho=\operatorname{id}_{M}\otimes\Delta
. A special case of this is when
M
is the
trivial
H
-module
, that is,
m\cdot h=\varepsilon(h)m
, for all
m\in M
and
h\in H
. In this situation,
M\otimes H
is called the
trivial Hopf module
.

Recall that the fundamental theorem of Hopf modules classify all Hopf modules as trivial, i.e., if
M
is a right-right
H
-Hopf module, then
M\cong M^{\operatorname{co}H}\otimes H
as right-right
H
-Hopf module, where
M^{\operatorname{co}H}\otimes H
has the trivial structure of Hopf module (e.g.
[
21
, Theorem 1.9.4]
).

2.5
(Co)module algebras

In this section we present (co)actions of Hopf algebras over algebras. Although most results and definitions presented are valid over bialgebras, throughout this part
H
will denote an arbitrary
K
-Hopf algebra (remember that we assume
H
flat over
K
).

Definition 2.22

(Module algebra, comodule algebra)
.

Let
A
be a
K
-algebra.

(i)

A
is a
left
H
-module algebra
if the following conditions hold:

(MA1)

A
is a left
H
-module,

(MA2)

For all
h\in H
and
a,b\in A
,

h\cdot(ab)=(h_{(1)}\cdot a)(h_{(2)}\cdot b)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad h\cdot 1_{A}=\varepsilon(h)1_{A}.

(6)

(ii)

A
is a
right
H
-comodule algebra
if the following conditions hold:

(CA1)

A
is a right
H
-comodule with structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes H
,

(CA2)

For all
a,b\in A
,

\rho(ab)=a_{(0)}b_{(0)}\otimes a_{(1)}b_{(1)}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\rho(1_{A})=1_{A}\otimes 1_{H}.

(7)

Condition
(i)(MA2)
is equivalent to
m_{A}
and
u_{A}
being
H
-module maps. Dually, condition
(ii)(CA2)
is equivalent to
m_{A}
and
u_{A}
being
H
-comodule maps, which is also equivalent to
\rho
being an algebra map (see e.g.
[
18
, Propositions 6.1.4 and 6.2.2]
). Also, as with previous concepts, we may define
right
H
-module algebras
,
left
H
-comodule algebras
and
(L,H)
-bi(co)module algebras
similarly.

Remark 2.23
.

The set of coinvariants in a comodule algebra is always a subalgebra, called the
subalgebra of coinvariants
. Indeed, if
A
is a right
H
-comodule algebra,
k\in K
and
a,b\in A^{\operatorname{co}H}
, then

\displaystyle\rho(a+b)=\rho(a)+\rho(b)=a\otimes 1+b\otimes 1=(a+b)\otimes 1,

\displaystyle\rho(ka)=k\rho(a)=k(a\otimes 1)=ka\otimes 1,

\displaystyle\rho(ab)=ab\otimes 1.

In the first two rows we used the linearity of
\rho
, while for the last one we used (
7
).

The next result extends Proposition
2.15
.

Proposition 2.24

(e.g.
[
18
, Proposition 6.2.4]
)
.

Let
H
be a finite-dimensional -Hopf algebra and
H^{*}
its dual Hopf algebra. Then, for a -algebra
A
, the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

A
is a right
H
-comodule algebra.

(ii)

A
is a left
H^{*}
-module algebra.

Moreover, under these conditions
A^{H^{*}}=A^{\operatorname{co}H}
.

Proof 2.25
.

Let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
be a basis for
H
and
\{e_{1}^{*},\ldots,e_{n}^{*}\}
the corresponding dual basis for
H^{*}
. If
A
is a right
H
-comodule algebra, then we already know that
A
is a
H^{*}
-module via (
4
). Moreover, if
a,b\in A
and
f\in H^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle f\cdot(ab)

\displaystyle=f((ab)_{(1)})(ab_{(0)})=f(a_{(1)}b_{(1)})a_{(0)}b_{(0)}=(fm_{H})(a_{(1)}\otimes b_{(1)})a_{(0)}b_{(0)}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\circ}(f)(a_{(1)}\otimes b_{(1)})a_{(0)}b_{(0)}=f_{(1)}(a_{(1)})f_{(2)}(b_{(1)})a_{(0)}b_{(0)}

\displaystyle=f_{(1)}(a_{(1)})a_{(0)}f_{(2)}(b_{(1)})b_{(0)}=(f_{(1)}\cdot a)(f_{(2)}\cdot b);

\displaystyle f\cdot 1_{A}

\displaystyle=f(1_{A})1_{A}=(fu_{H})(1_{H})1_{A}=u^{\circ}(f)1_{A}.

Therefore,
A
is a left
H^{*}
-module algebra.

Conversely, let
A
be a left
H^{*}
-module algebra. We already know that
A
is a
H
-comodule via (
5
). Moreover, if
a,b\in A
and
f\in H^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes f)(\rho(ab))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{*}\cdot(ab)\otimes f(e_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(e_{i}^{*}\cdot(ab))f(e_{i})\otimes 1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(e_{i}^{*}f(e_{i}))\cdot(ab)\otimes 1

\displaystyle=f\cdot(ab)\otimes 1=(f_{(1)}\cdot a)(f_{(2)}\cdot b)\otimes 1=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}((e_{i}^{*}f_{(1)}(e_{i}))\cdot a)((e_{j}^{*}f_{(2)}(e_{j}))\cdot b)\otimes 1

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(e_{i}^{*}\cdot a)(e_{j}^{*}\cdot b)\otimes f_{(1)}(e_{i})f_{(2)}(e_{j})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(e_{i}^{*}\cdot a)(e_{j}^{*}\cdot b)\otimes f(e_{i}e_{j})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes f)\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}(e_{i}^{*}\cdot a)(e_{j}^{*}\cdot b)\otimes e_{i}e_{j}\right)=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes f)(\rho(a)\rho(b)),

and hence
\rho(ab)=\rho(a)\rho(b)
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle\rho(1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{*}\cdot 1_{A}\otimes e_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{*}(1_{H})1_{A}\otimes e_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}1_{A}\otimes e_{i}^{*}(1_{H})e_{i}=1_{A}\otimes\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{*}(1_{H})e_{i}=1_{A}\otimes 1_{H}.

Thus,
A
is a right
H
-comodule algebra.

The equality
A^{H^{*}}=A^{\operatorname{co}H}
follows as in the proof of Proposition
2.15
.

Now, we generalize the notion of Hopf module by replacing the module structure over
H
(or a Hopf subalgebra
L
) by a comodule algebra.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
A
be a right
H
-comodule algebra. A
K
-module
M
is said to be a
left-right
(A,H)
-Hopf module
if the following conditions hold:

(HM1)

M
is a left
A
-module,

(HM2)

M
is a right
H
-comodule with structure map
\rho_{M}:M\rightarrow M\otimes H
,

(HM3)

For every
a\in A
and
m\in M
,
\rho_{M}(a\cdot m)=a_{(0)}\cdot m_{(0)}\otimes a_{(1)}m_{(1)}
.

The category of left-right
(A,H)
-Hopf modules,
{}_{A}{\operatorname{Mod}}^{H}
, has as morphisms the
K
-linear maps which are also
A
-linear and
H
-colinear. Similarly, objects in the category
{\operatorname{Mod}}^{H}_{A}
can be define by replacing
(HM3)
with
\rho_{M}(m\cdot a)=m_{(0)}\cdot a_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}a_{(1)}
.

We end this section by giving some examples characterizing actions and coactions of distinguished Hopf algebras. We assume that the base ring is a field .

Example 2.27

((Co)actions of a Hopf algebra over itself)
.

It is clear that
H
is a right
H
-comodule algebra using
\rho=\Delta
. By Proposition
2.24
, when
H
if finite dimensional, this dualizes to a left action (denoted by
\rightharpoonup
) of
H^{*}
on
H
given by

f\rightharpoonup h=f(h_{(2)})h_{(1)},\qquad\mbox{for all }h\in H\mbox{ and }f\in H^{*}.

The (co)invariants are given by
H^{H^{*}}=H^{\operatorname{co}H}=1
.

Example 2.28

(Actions of the group algebra)
.

Let
G
be a group. We say that
G

acts (from the left) as automorphisms
on a -algebra
A
if there is a group morphism
\psi:G\rightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{K-\operatorname{Alg}}(A)
. In this case, we write
\psi(g)(a)=g(a)
(or
g^{a}
), for all
g\in G
and
a\in A
. Moreover, if
\psi
is injective, we say that
G
acts
faithfully
.

If
G
acts as automorphisms on a
A
and
G
is the group algebra of
G
(see Example
2.6
), then
A
is a left
G
-module algebra via
g\cdot a=g(a)
, for all
g\in G
and
a\in A
. Indeed, for every
g\in G
and
a,b\in A
, we have

\displaystyle g\cdot(ab)=g(ab)=g(a)g(b)=(g\cdot a)(g\cdot b),

\displaystyle g\cdot 1_{A}=g(1_{A})=1_{A}=1_{A}1_{A}=\varepsilon(g)1_{A}.

In this case,
A^{G}
is the set of fixed points under the action of
G
,

A^{G}=A^{G}:=\{a\in A:g(a)=a,\,\forall g\in G\}.

Conversely, if
A
is a
G
-module algebra, then
G
acts as automorphisms on
A
via the map
\psi:G\rightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{K-\operatorname{Alg}}(A)
, given by
\psi(g)(a)=g\cdot a
.

Thus, we have shown that
A
is a
G
-module algebra if and only if
G
acts as automorphisms on
A
.

Example 2.29

(Coactions of the group algebra)
.

Let
G
be a group and let
A
be a -algebra. We say that
A
is a
G
-graded algebra
if there exists a collection
\{A_{g}\}_{g\in G}
of -subspaces of
A
such that

A=\bigoplus_{g\in G}A_{g}
and
A_{g}A_{h}\subseteq A_{gh}
, for all
g,h\in G
.

If
A
is a
G
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes G
, then we replace the Heyneman–Sweedler notation by writing instead

\rho(a)=\sum a_{g}\otimes g
, for all
a\in A
. By definition,
[(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\Delta)\rho](a)=[(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{G})\rho](a)
, for all
a\in A
. Expanding the left-hand side we get

[(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\Delta)\rho](a)=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\Delta)\left(\sum a_{g}\otimes g\right)=\sum a_{g}\otimes g\otimes g,

while the right-hand side gives

[(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{G})\rho](a)=(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{G})\left(\sum a_{g}\otimes g\right)=\sum(a_{g})_{h}\otimes h\otimes g.

Comparing both expressions we have that

(a_{g})_{h}=\begin{cases}a_{g}&\mbox{if } \mbox{$g=h$},\\
0&\mbox{if } \mbox{$g\neq h$}.\end{cases}

Thus
\rho(a_{g})=a_{g}\otimes g
and we can set
A_{g}=\{a_{g}:a\in A\}
, for all
g\in G
. Since
\rho
is -linear we have
\rho(a+b)=\rho(a)+\rho(b)
and
\rho(ka)=k\rho(a)
, for all
a,b\in A
and
k\in
. Thus,

(a+b)_{g}=a_{g}+b_{g}
and
(ka)_{g}=ka_{g}
, and therefore
A_{g}
is a -subspace of
A
, for all
g\in G
. On the other hand, since
G
is a basis of
G
, the sum
\sum_{g\in G}A_{g}
is direct. Indeed, if
0=a_{g_{1}}+\cdots+a_{g_{t}}
for some
a_{g_{k}}\in A_{g_{k}}
,
1\leq k\leq t
, by applying
\rho
we get

0\otimes 0=\rho(0)=\rho(a_{g_{1}}+\cdots+a_{g_{t}})=\rho(a_{g_{1}})+\cdots+\rho(a_{g_{t}})=a_{g_{1}}\otimes g_{1}+\cdots+a_{g_{t}}\otimes g_{t},

and thus
a_{g_{k}}=0
, for
1\leq k\leq t
. Additionally, since
A
is a
G
-comodule algebra,

\rho(a_{g}b_{h})=a_{g}b_{h}\otimes gh,\qquad\mbox{for all }a_{g}\in A_{g},\,b_{h}\in A_{h}.

Therefore
a_{g}b_{h}\in A_{gh}
and
A_{g}A_{h}\subseteq A_{gh}
. Finally, by definition
[(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon)\rho](a)=a\otimes 1
, for all
a\in A
. But the left hand side is

[(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon)\rho](a)=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\varepsilon)\left(\sum a_{g}\otimes g\right)=\sum a_{g}\otimes 1,

and thus for each
a\in A
, we have
\sum a_{g}=a
. Therefore
A=\bigoplus_{g\in G}A_{g}
and
A
is a
G
-graded algebra. Notice that

A^{\operatorname{co}H}=\{a\in A:\rho(a)=a\otimes 1\}=A_{1},

the identity component of the
G
-graduation. In particular,
1_{A}\in A_{1}
.

Conversely, if
A
is a
G
-graded algebra define the structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes G
as
\rho(a)=a\otimes g
, for all
a\in A_{g}
and
g\in G
.

Thus, we have shown that
A
is a
G
-comodule algebra if and only if
A
is a
G
-graded algebra.

Example 2.30

(Actions of the dual group algebra)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and recall from Example
2.7
that
(G)^{*}=^{G}
. By Proposition
2.24
, the actions of
G
correspond to coactions of
G
, which by Example
2.29
are precisely
G
-gradings.

Example 2.31

(Actions of the universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra)
.

Let
A
be a -algebra. A -linear map
\delta:A\to A
is a
derivation
if
\delta(ab)=a\delta(b)+\delta(a)b
, for all
a,b\in A
. Then the space of -derivations over
A
,
\operatorname{Der}(A)
, becomes a Lie algebra by taking as Lie bracket the commutator, i.e.,
[\delta,\pi]=\delta\pi-\pi\delta
, for all
\delta,\pi\in\operatorname{Der}(A)
. Indeed, for
a,b\in A
:

\displaystyle(\delta\pi-\pi\delta)(ab)

\displaystyle=\delta(\pi(ab))-\pi(\delta(ab))=\delta(a\pi(b))+\delta(\pi(a)b)-\pi(a\delta(b))-\pi(\delta(a)b)

\displaystyle=a\delta(\pi(b))+\delta(a)\pi(b)+\pi(a)\delta(b)+\delta(\pi(a))b

\displaystyle\quad-a\pi(\delta(b))-\pi(a)\delta(b)-\delta(a)\pi(b)-\pi(\delta(a))b

\displaystyle=a(\delta\pi-\pi\delta)(b)+(\delta\pi-\pi\delta)(a)b.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra. We say that
\mathfrak{g}

acts by derivations
on
A
if there exists a Lie algebra morphism
\psi:\mathfrak{g}\to\operatorname{Der}(A)
.

If
\mathfrak{g}
acts by derivations on
A
and
U(\mathfrak{g})
is the universal enveloping algebra of
G
(see Example
2.10
), then
A
is a left
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module algebra via
x\cdot a=\psi(x)(a)
, for all
x\in\mathfrak{g}
and
a\in A
(extended by the PBW theorem). In this case,

A^{U(\mathfrak{g})}:=A^{\mathfrak{g}}=\{a\in A:x\cdot a=0,\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathfrak{g}\}.

Conversely, if
A
is a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module algebra, using (
6
) we get
x\cdot(ab)=x\cdot(a)b+a(x\cdot b)
and
x\cdot 1=0
. This implies that the map
\psi:\mathfrak{g}\to\operatorname{Der}(A)
given by
\psi(x)(a):=x\cdot a
is a Lie algebra morphism and thus
\mathfrak{g}
acts by derivations on
A
.

Hence, we have shown that
A
is a
U(\mathfrak{g})
-module algebra if and only if
\mathfrak{g}
acts by derivations on
A
.

2.6
Hopf Galois extensions

Finally, we are able to introduce one of the transversal concepts appearing in this document. Hopf Galois extensions generalize the notion of Galois extensions over rings, replacing the action of a group on the algebra by the coaction of a Hopf algebra. The first general definition is due to Kreimer and Takeuchi
[
3
]
, although the commutative case was previously studied by Chase and Sweedler
[
2
]
. As in the previous section, throughout the discussion
H
will denote an arbitrary
K
-Hopf algebra.

Definition 2.32

(
H
-Galois extension)
.

Let
A
be a right
H
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes H
. We say that the extension
A^{\operatorname{co}H}\subset A
(also denoted
A/A^{\operatorname{co}H}
) is a
right
H
-Galois extension
if the map
\beta:A\otimes_{A^{\operatorname{co}H}}A\rightarrow A\otimes H
, given by

\beta(a\otimes b)=(a\otimes 1)\rho(b)=ab_{(0)}\otimes b_{(1)},\qquad\mbox{for all }a,b\in A,

(8)

is bijective. In this case
\beta
is called the
Galois map
.

Notice that we could have defined the Galois map as
\beta^{\prime}:A\otimes_{A^{\operatorname{co}H}}A\rightarrow A\otimes H
given by

\beta^{\prime}(a\otimes b)=\rho(a)(b\otimes 1)=a_{(0)}b\otimes a_{(1)},\qquad\mbox{for all }a,b\in A.

(9)

A natural question is whether
\beta^{\prime}
can replace
\beta
. The following result gives a case in which the answer is affirmative.

Proposition 2.33

(e.g.
[
22
, p. 372]
)
.

Let
A
be a right
H
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes H
. If the antipode
S
is bijective, then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

The map
\beta
, given by (
8
), is bijective (resp. injective, surjective).

(ii)

The map
\beta^{\prime}
, given by (
9
), is bijective (resp. injective, surjective).

Proof 2.34
.

Let
\Phi:A\otimes H\rightarrow A\otimes H
the endomorphism given by

\Phi(a\otimes h):=\rho(a)(1\otimes S(h))=a_{(0)}\otimes a_{(1)}S(h),\qquad\mbox{for all }a\in A,\,h\in H.

We have, for all
a,b\in A
,

\displaystyle(\Phi\beta)(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(ab_{(0)}\otimes b_{(1)}\right)=\Phi(ab_{(0)}\otimes b_{(1)})=\rho(ab_{(0)})(1\otimes S(b_{(1)}))

\displaystyle=(a_{(0)}b_{(0)}\otimes a_{(1)}b_{(1)})(1\otimes S(b_{(2)}))=a_{(0)}b_{(0)}\otimes a_{(1)}b_{(1)}S(b_{(2)})

\displaystyle=a_{(0)}b_{(0)}\otimes a_{(1)}\varepsilon(b_{(1)})=a_{(0)}b_{(0)}\varepsilon(b_{(1)})\otimes a_{(1)}

\displaystyle=a_{(0)}b\otimes a_{(1)}=\beta^{\prime}(a\otimes b).

Hence
\Phi\beta=\beta^{\prime}
. But notice that
\Phi
has as inverse
\Phi^{-1}:A\otimes H\rightarrow A\otimes H
given by

\Phi^{-1}(a\otimes h):=(1\otimes S^{-1}(h))\rho(a)=a_{(0)}\otimes S^{-1}(h)a_{(1)},\qquad\mbox{for all }a\in A,\,h\in H.

Therefore, the assertions are equivalent.

Similarly,
left
H
-Galois extensions
can be defined with Galois map
\beta_{l}:A\otimes_{A^{\operatorname{co}H}}A\rightarrow H\otimes A
. If several Hopf Galois extensions of different laterality are involved, we add to their Galois maps an index indicating whether these are left or right sided; for example, for a right Hopf Galois extension we may write
\beta_{r}
.

When a right
H
-Galois extension is of the form
K\subset A
(i.e.,
A^{\operatorname{co}H}=K
), we call
A
a
right
H
-Galois object
. In this situation, the Galois map is given by the composition

\beta:\hbox to210.7pt{\vbox to17.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 105.34695pt\lower-7.49304pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Moreover, we have the following useful result.

Proposition 2.35

(
[
2
, Theorem 1.15]
)
.

Let
A
be a right
H
-Galois object. Then
A
is a faithfully flat
K
-module.

As with bi(co)modules, the notion of Galois objects may be two-sided with certain compatibility.

Definition 2.36

(BiGalois object)
.

Let
H,L
be two
K
-Hopf algebras. A
K
-algebra
A
is a
(L,H)
-biGalois object
if the following assertions hold:

(BG1)

A
is a faithfully flat
(L,H)
-bicomodule algebra,

(BG2)

A
is a left
L
-Galois extension of
K
,

(BG3)

A
is a right
H
-Galois extension of
K
.

2.7
Examples of Hopf Galois extensions

In this section we review a large amount of examples of Hopf Galois extensions. Unless otherwise stated, they are adapted from
[
18
,
21
,
22
,
24
]
.

2.7.1
Hopf algebras

Every
K
-Hopf algebra
H
can be seen as a
K
-Galois object. Indeed, since
H
has a natural structure of
H
-comodule algebra via the comultiplication (i.e., the structure map is
\rho:=\Delta
), for any
h\in H^{\operatorname{co}H}
,

\rho(h)=\Delta(h)=h_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}=h\otimes 1.

Applying
\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H}
we have

(\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})(h_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)})=(\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})(h\otimes 1),

whence
\varepsilon(h_{(1)})1\otimes h_{(2)}=\varepsilon(h)1\otimes 1
, which via the isomorphism
K\otimes H\cong H
, gives

h=\varepsilon(h_{(1)})h_{(2)}=\varepsilon(h)1,

that is,
h\in K
. Conversely, if
h\in K
then
\Delta(h)=\Delta(h1)=h\Delta(1)=h(1\otimes 1)=h\otimes 1
and therefore
h\in H^{\operatorname{co}H}
. Hence
H^{\operatorname{co}H}=K
.

The map
\beta:H\otimes H\rightarrow H\otimes H
is defined by

\beta(a\otimes b)=(a\otimes 1)\rho(b)=(a\otimes 1)\Delta(b)=ab_{(1)}\otimes b_{(2)},\qquad\mbox{for all }a,b\in H.

Then, the inverse
\beta^{-1}:H\otimes H\rightarrow H\otimes H
of the Galois map is given by

\beta^{-1}(a\otimes b)=aS(b_{(1)})\otimes b_{(2)},\qquad\mbox{for all }a,b\in H.

Indeed, if
a,b\in H
, then

\displaystyle\beta(\beta^{-1}(a\otimes b))

\displaystyle=\beta\left(aS(b_{(1)})\otimes b_{(2)}\right)=\beta(aS(b_{(1)})\otimes b_{(2)})=aS(b_{(1)})b_{(2)}\otimes b_{(3)}

\displaystyle=a\varepsilon(b_{(1)})\otimes b_{(2)}=a\otimes\varepsilon(b_{(1)})b_{(2)}=a\otimes b;

\displaystyle\beta^{-1}(\beta(a\otimes b))

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}\left(ab_{(1)}\otimes b_{(2)}\right)=\beta^{-1}(ab_{(1)}\otimes b_{(2)})=ab_{(1)}S(b_{(2)})\otimes b_{(3)}

\displaystyle=a\varepsilon(b_{(1)})\otimes b_{(2)}=a\otimes\varepsilon(b_{(1)})b_{(2)}=a\otimes b.

Therefore,
\beta
is bijective and we have shown the next result.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra. Then
K\subset H
is a
H
-Galois extension.

Remark 2.38
.

Since Hopf Galois extensions are definable over bialgebras, it can be shown for every bialgebra
H
that, in fact,
H
is a Hopf algebra if and only if
K\subset H
is a
H
-Galois extension
[
24
, Example 2.1.2]
. More generally, if
H
is a
K
-flat bialgebra admitting a
H
-Galois extension
A^{\operatorname{co}H}\subset A
that is faithfully flat as a
K
-module,
H
must be a Hopf algebra
[
32
]
.

2.7.2
Classical field extensions

Let
G
be a finite group acting as automorphisms on a field
E\supset
in the sense of Example
2.28
. Clearly
E
is a left
G
-module algebra. Hence, by Proposition
2.24
, it is a right
G
-comodule algebra (recall that
(G)^{*}=^{G}
; see Examples
2.7
and
2.30
). As the name suggest, the next result shows that classical Galois extensions of fields can be seen as Hopf Galois extensions.

Theorem 2.39

(e.g.
[
21
, Section 8.1.2]
)
.

Let
G
be a finite group acting as automorphisms on a field
E\supset
, and
F:=E^{G}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

The field extension
F\subset E
is Galois with Galois group
G
.

(ii)

G
acts faithfully on
E
.

(iii)

[E:F]=|G|
.

(iv)

F\subset E
is a right
G
-Galois extension.

Proof 2.40
.

The implications (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii) are consequences of Artin’s Lemma (e.g.
[
33
, Theorem 4.7]
). Hence, we will just show their equivalence with (iv). Suppose that the field extension
F\subset E
is Galois. Set
n:=|G|
and
G=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. Let
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\}
be a basis of
E/F
and let
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}\subset^{G}
be the dual basis to
\{x_{i}\}\subset G
. As it was said before,
E
coacts on
G
, where the structure map
\rho:E\rightarrow E\otimes^{G}
is given by

\rho(a)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}\cdot a)\otimes p_{i},\qquad\mbox{for all }a\in E.

Therefore, the Galois map
\beta:E\otimes_{F}E\rightarrow E\otimes^{G}
is given by

\beta(a\otimes b)=(a\otimes 1)\rho(b)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a(x_{i}\cdot b)\otimes p_{i}.

We must show that
\beta
is bijective. For that, suppose
w=\sum_{j}a_{j}\otimes u_{j}\in\ker(\beta)
. Then

\beta\left(w\right)=\sum_{j}a_{j}(x_{i}\cdot u_{j})\otimes p_{i}=0\otimes 0.

Since the
p_{i}
are linearly independent, we conclude that

\sum_{j}a_{j}(x_{i}\cdot u_{j})=0,\qquad\mbox{for all }1\leq i\leq n.

(10)

Using the faithfulness of the action of
G
, Dedekind independence theorem implies that the
n\times n
matrix
C=[x_{i}\cdot u_{j}]
associated to the system (
10
) is invertible (e.g.
[
33
, p. 291]
). Hence, all
a_{j}
are
0
and
w=0
. Thus
\beta
is injective. Since both
E\otimes_{F}E
and
E\otimes^{G}
are
F
-vector spaces of dimension
n^{2}
, it follows that
\beta
is a bijection.

Conversely, suppose that the Galois map
\beta
is an isomorphism. Notice that

\dim_{F}(E\otimes_{F}E)=[E:F]^{2}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\dim_{F}(E\otimes^{G})=[E:F]|G|.

Via the isomorphism, we get
[E:F]=|G|
and therefore
F\subset E
is a Galois extension of fields.

2.7.3
Separable field extensions

For the circle Hopf algebra
H
(cf. Example
2.14
), it is possible for a finite separable field extension
F\subset F
to be
H
-Galois, although it is not Galois in the classical sense. This example is due to Greither and Pareigis
[
34
]
.

Let
=F=\mathbb{Q}
and
E=F(\omega)
, where
\omega
is the real
4
-th root of 2.
F\subset E
is not Galois for any group
G
, since the automorphism group of
E/F
fixes
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
pointwise. However, if
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the circle Hopf algebra, it can be shown that
F\subset E
is
H_{\mathbb{Q}}^{*}
-Galois. In this case
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
acts on
E
as follows:

\cdot

1

\omega

\omega^{2}

\omega^{3}

c

1

0

-\omega^{2}

0

s

0

-\omega

0

\omega^{3}

When we change for
\mathbb{Q}(i)
,
H_{\cong}\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, the group algebra of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
. This means that
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{Z}_{4}
and
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
are
\mathbb{Q}(i)
-forms of each other. For a suitable Hopf algebra
H
described in
[
35
, Section 3]
, which is a
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-2})
-form of
\mathbb{Q}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
,
\mathbb{Q}\subset E
is also a
H^{*}
-Galois. Hence an extension can be Hopf Galois over two different Hopf algebras.

2.7.4

Strongly graded algebras

This example is due to Ulbrich and Osterburg
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
. Let
A=\bigoplus_{g\in G}A_{g}
be a
G
-graded algebra (see Example
2.29
).
A
is
strongly graded
if
A_{g}A_{h}=A_{gh}
, for all
g,h\in G
.

Lemma 2.41

(e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 1.1.1]
)
.

Let
A
be a
G
-graded algebra. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

A
is strongly graded,

(ii)

A_{g}A_{g^{-1}}=A_{1}
for all
g\in G
.

Proof 2.42
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) is trivial. (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): We have

A_{gh}=A_{gh}A_{1}=A_{gh}(A_{h^{-1}}A_{h})\subseteq(A_{gh}A_{h}^{-1})A_{h}\subseteq A_{ghh^{-1}}A_{h}=A_{g}A_{h}\subseteq A_{gh},

for all
g,h\in G
.

Recall that
A
is a
G
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes G
given by
\rho(a)=a\otimes g
, for all
a\in A_{g}
, and that
A^{coG}=A_{1}
. Then
\beta:A\otimes_{A_{1}}A\rightarrow A\otimes G
is given by
\beta(a\otimes b)=(a\otimes 1)\rho(b)=\sum_{g\in G}ab_{g}\otimes g
, for all
a,b\in A
with
b=\sum_{g\in G}b_{g}
.

Theorem 2.43

(e.g.
[
21
, Theorem 8.1.7]
)
.

Let
G
be a group and
A
be a
G
-graded algebra. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(i)

A_{1}\subset A
is a
G
-extension.

(ii)

A
is strongly graded.

Proof 2.44
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): First assume that
\beta
is bijective and, in particular, surjective. Whence for every
g\in G
there exist finitely many
a_{i},b_{i}\in A
such that

\beta\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}\sum_{h\in G}a_{i}(b_{i})_{h}\otimes h=\sum_{i,h}a_{i}(b_{i})_{h}\otimes h=1\otimes g.

Since
G
is a basis for
G
, it follows that
\sum_{i}a_{i}(b_{i})_{g}=1
and
\sum_{i}a_{i}(b_{i})_{h}=0
for all
h\neq g
. The inclusion
A_{g}A_{g^{-1}}\subseteq A_{1}
always holds in
G
-graded algebras, so now suppose
a\in A_{1}
. Fixing
g
, we have
a=a1=a\sum_{i}a_{i}(b_{i})_{g}=\sum_{i}aa_{i}(b_{i})_{g}
. Since the
(b_{i})_{g}
are homogeneous of degree
g
,
a
is homogeneous of degree 1, and since the sum of homogeneous components is direct, we get
a_{i}\in A_{g^{-1}}
. Hence
A_{g}A_{g^{-1}}=A_{1}
and by Lemma
2.41
it follows that
A
is strongly graded.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Conversely, suppose now that
A
is strongly graded. If
g\in G
, then by Lemma
2.41
,
1\in A_{1}=A_{g^{-1}}A_{g}
and we may write
1=\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}
for some
a_{i}\in A_{g^{-1}}
and
b_{i}\in A_{g}
. Therefore let
\alpha:A\otimes G\rightarrow A\otimes_{A_{1}}A
be defined as
\alpha(a\otimes g)=\sum_{i}aa_{i}\otimes b_{i}
.
Since all
g_{i}\in A_{g}
, we get

(\beta\alpha)(a\otimes g)=\beta\left(\sum_{i}aa_{i}\otimes b_{i}\right)=\sum_{i}aa_{i}b_{i}\otimes g=a\left(a_{i}b_{i}\right)\otimes g=a1\otimes g=a\otimes g.

On the other hand,

(\alpha\beta)(a\otimes b)=\alpha\left(\sum_{g\in G}ab_{g}\otimes g\right)=\sum_{g\in G}\alpha(ab_{g}\otimes g)=\sum_{g\in G}\sum_{i}ab_{g}a_{i}\otimes b_{i},

but
b_{g}a_{i}\in A_{g}A_{g^{-1}}=A_{1}
and hence

\displaystyle(\alpha\beta)(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in G}\sum_{i}ab_{g}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}=\sum_{g\in G}\sum_{i}a\otimes b_{g}a_{i}b_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in G}a\otimes b_{g}\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}\right)=\sum_{g\in G}a\otimes b_{g}1=a\otimes b.

Therefore,
\alpha=\beta^{-1}
and
\beta
is bijective.

2.7.5

Crossed products over groups

We now give a major example that can be deduced from the previous one. Let
G
be a (multiplicative) group acting as automorphisms on a -algebra
R
. Recall that we denoted
g(r)=g\cdot r
. The action is said to be
twisted
if there exists a map
\sigma:G\times G\rightarrow R
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

(
Cocycle condition
) For all
g,h,k\in G
,
[g\cdot\sigma(h,k)]\sigma(h,hk)=\sigma(g,h)\sigma(gh,k)
, and
\sigma(g,1)=\sigma(1,g)=1

(ii)

(
Twisted module condition
) For all
g,h\in G
and
r\in R
,

[g\cdot(h\cdot r)]\sigma(g,h)=\sigma(g,h)(gh\cdot r).

In this case, we say that
\sigma
is a
2-cocycle
.

We define a new different structure over
R\otimes G
. In this context, an arbitrary element
r\otimes g\in R\otimes G
will be denoted by
r*g
.

Definition 2.45

(Crossed product)
.

Let
G
be a group acting as automorphisms on a -algebra
R
. If the action is twisted, we define the
crossed product of
R
and
G
, denoted by
R*G
, as the -space
R\otimes G
together with the multiplication

(r*g)(s*h)=r(g\cdot s)\sigma(g,h)*gh,\qquad\mbox{for all }r,s\in R\mbox{ and }g,h\in G,

and unit element
1_{R}*1_{G}
.

Proposition 2.46

(e.g.
[
22
, Example 2.7]
)
.

Let
G
be a group acting as automorphisms on a -algebra
R
. Suppose that the action is twisted. Then
R*G
is a
G
-graded algebra. Moreover,
R\subset A
is a
G
-Galois extension.

Proof 2.47
.

For all
g,h,k\in G
and
r,s,t\in R
we have

\displaystyle(r*g)[(s*h)(t*k)]

\displaystyle=(r*g)(s(h\cdot t)\sigma(h,k)*hk)

\displaystyle=r\left[g\cdot(s(h\cdot t)\sigma(h,k))\right]\sigma(g,hk)*g(hk)

\displaystyle=r\left[(g\cdot s)(g\cdot(h\cdot t))(g\cdot\sigma(h,k))\right]\sigma(g,hk)*g(hk)

\displaystyle=r(g\cdot s)(g\cdot(h\cdot t))\left[(g\cdot\sigma(h,k))\sigma(g,hk)\right]*(gh)k

\displaystyle=r(g\cdot s)(g\cdot(h\cdot t))\left[\sigma(g,h)\sigma(gh,k)\right]*(gh)k

\displaystyle=r(g\cdot s)\left[(g\cdot(h\cdot t))\sigma(g,h)\right]\sigma(gh,k)*(gh)k

\displaystyle=r(g\cdot s)\left[\sigma(g,h)(gh\cdot t)\right]\sigma(gh,k)*(gh)k

\displaystyle=\left[r(g\cdot s)\sigma(g,h)*gh\right](t*k)=\left[(r*g)(s*h)\right](t*k).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(r*g)(1*1)

\displaystyle=r(g\cdot 1)\sigma(g,1)*g1=r1*g1=r*g,

\displaystyle(1*1)(r*g)

\displaystyle=1(1\cdot r)\sigma(1,g)*1g=1r1*1g=r*g.

Hence,
R*G
is a -algebra. The homogeneous components are given by

(R*G)_{1}=R\otimes 1\cong R\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(R*G)_{g}=R\otimes g,\quad\mbox{for all }g\in G.

Finally, since
(R*G)_{g}(R*G)_{h}=R\otimes gh=(R*G)_{gh}
, for all
g,h\in G
, the algebra is strongly graded and therefore, by Theorem
2.43
,
R\subset A
is
G
-Galois.

The relation between crossed products over groups and group extensions is explored in
[
22
, Example 2.8]
by showing that for any group
G
with normal subgroup
N
and quotient
L=G/N
,
G=N*L
. Also, in
[
22
, Examples 2.9 and 2.10]
counterexamples of strongly graded algebras that are not crossed products are given.

2.7.6

Hopf crossed products and smash products

This example attempts to generalize the previous one to the context of Hopf algebras. Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra and
R
an
H
-module algebra. The action is said to be
twisted
if there exists a map
\sigma:H\times H\rightarrow R
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

(
Hopf 2-cocycle condition
) For all
g,h,k\in H
,

\displaystyle[g_{(1)}\cdot\sigma(h_{(1)},k_{(1)})]\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(2)}k_{(2)})=\sigma(g_{(1)},h_{(1)})\sigma(g_{(2)}h_{(2)},k),

(11)

\displaystyle\sigma(g,1)=\sigma(1,g)=\varepsilon(g)1.

(ii)

(
Hopf twisted module condition
) For all
g,h\in H
and
r\in R
,

[g\cdot(h\cdot r)]=\sigma(g_{(1)},h_{(1)})(g_{(2)}h_{(2)}\cdot r)\sigma(g_{(3)},h_{(3)})^{-1}.

(12)

In this case, we say that
\sigma
is a
2-cocycle
.

Definition 2.48

(Hopf crossed product)
.

Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra and
R
an
H
-module algebra. If the action is twisted, we define the
crossed product of
R
and
H
, denoted by
R\#_{\sigma}H
, as the
K
-module
R\otimes H
together with the multiplication

(r\#g)(s\#h)=r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})\#g_{(3)}h_{(2)},\quad\mbox{for all }r,s\in R\mbox{ and }g,h\in H,

(13)

and unit element
1_{R}\#1_{H}
.

For any group
G
, if
H=G
, then this definition coincides with that of a crossed product over
G
(see Definition
2.45
).

Theorem 2.49

(e.g.
[
22
, Example 3.6]
)
.

Let
R
be an
H
-module algebra. Suppose that the action is twisted. If
A:=R\#_{\sigma}H
, then:

(i)

A
is an algebra.

(ii)

A
is a right
H
-comodule algebra via
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes H
given by

\rho(r\#h)=(r\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)},\qquad\mbox{for all }r\#h\in A.

(iii)

A^{\operatorname{co}H}\cong R
.

Proof 2.50
.

(i) For all
r,s,t\in R
and
g,h,f\in H
, we have

\displaystyle(r\#g)\left[(s\#h)(t\#f)\right]=(r\#g)\left[s(h_{(1)}\cdot t)\sigma(h_{(2)},f_{(1)})\#h_{(3)}f_{(2)}\right]

\displaystyle=r(g_{(1)}\cdot(s(h_{(1)}\cdot t)\sigma(h_{(2)},f_{(1)})))\sigma(g_{(2)},(h_{(3)}f_{(2)})_{(2)})\#g_{(3)}(h_{(3)}f_{(2)})_{(2)}

\displaystyle=r(g_{(1)}\cdot(s(h_{(1)}\cdot t)\sigma(h_{(2)},f_{(1)})))\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(4)}f_{(3)})\#g_{(3)}h_{(4)}f_{(3)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e11}}}{=}r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)(g_{(2)}\cdot(h_{(1)}\cdot t))(g_{(3)}\cdot\sigma(h_{(2)},f_{(1)}))\sigma(g_{(4)},h_{(4)}f_{(3)})\#g_{(5)}h_{(4)}f_{(3)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e81}}}{=}r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)(g_{(2)}\cdot(h_{(1)}\cdot t))\sigma(g_{(3)},h_{(2)})\sigma(g_{(4)}h_{(3)},f_{(1)})\#g_{(5)}h_{(4)}f_{(2)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e82}}}{=}r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})(g_{(3)}h_{(2)}\cdot t)\sigma(g_{(4)}h_{(3)},f_{(1)})\#g_{(5)}h_{(4)}f_{(2)}

\displaystyle=r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})((g_{(3)}h_{(2)})_{(1)}\cdot t)\sigma((g_{(3)}h_{(2)})_{(2)},f_{(2)})\#(g_{(3)}h_{(2)})_{(3)}f_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left[r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})\#g_{(3)}h_{(2)}\right](t\#f)=\left[(r\#g)(s\#h)\right](t\#f)

and

\displaystyle(r\#g)(1\#1)

\displaystyle=r(g_{(1)}\cdot 1)\sigma(g_{(2)},{(1)})\#g_{(3)}\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e82}}}{=}(g_{1}\cdot 1)\varepsilon(g_{(2)})\#g_{(3)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e11}}}{=}\varepsilon(g_{(1)})\varepsilon(g_{(2)})\#g_{(3)}=r\#\varepsilon(g_{(1)}g_{(2)})g_{(3)}=r\#g,

\displaystyle(1\#1)(r\#g)

\displaystyle=1(1\cdot r)\sigma(1,g_{(1)})\#1g_{(2)}\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e82}}}{=}r\varepsilon(g_{(1)})\#g_{(2)}=r\#\varepsilon(g_{(1)})g_{(2)}=r\#g.

Therefore,
R\#_{\sigma}H
is an algebra.

(ii) Since for all
r\in R
and
h\in H
we have

\displaystyle[(\operatorname{id}_{R\#_{\sigma}H}\otimes\Delta)\rho](r\#h)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{R\#_{\sigma}H}\otimes\Delta)\left((r\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)}\right)=(a\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)}\otimes h_{(3)}

\displaystyle=(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})\left((r\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)}\right)=[(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})\rho](r\#h),

\displaystyle[(\operatorname{id}_{R\#_{\sigma}H}\otimes\varepsilon)\rho](r\#h)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{R\#_{\sigma}H}\otimes\varepsilon)\left((r\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)}\right)=(r\#h_{(1)})\otimes\varepsilon(h_{(2)})1

\displaystyle=r\#h_{(1)}\varepsilon(h_{(2)})\otimes 1=r\#h\otimes 1,

it follows that
R\#_{\sigma}H
is an
H
-module. Moreover, for all
r,s\in R
and
g,h\in H
,

\displaystyle\rho((r\#g)(s\#h))

\displaystyle=\rho\left(r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})\#g_{(3)}h_{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=(r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})\#(g_{(3)}h_{(2)})_{(1)})\otimes(g_{(3)}h_{(2)})_{(2)}

\displaystyle=(r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\sigma(g_{(2)},h_{(1)})\#g_{(3)}h_{(2)}\otimes g_{(4)}h_{(3)}

\displaystyle=(r\#g_{(1)})(s\#h_{(1)})\otimes g_{(2)}h_{(2)},

\displaystyle\rho(1\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=(1\#1)\otimes 1,

meaning that
R\#_{\sigma}H
is an
H
-comodule algebra.

(iii) We have

(R\#_{\sigma}H)^{\operatorname{co}H}=\left\{z\in R\#_{\sigma}H:\rho(z)=z\otimes 1\right\}.

Clearly,
R\cong R\#_{\sigma}1\subseteq(R\#_{\sigma}H)^{\operatorname{co}H}
. Reciprocally, if
r\#h\in(R\#_{\sigma}H)^{\operatorname{co}H}
, applying the map
\operatorname{id}_{R}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H}
to the equality
\rho(r\#h)=(r\#h)\otimes 1
we get at the left-hand side

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{R}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})(\rho(r\#h))

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{R}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})\left((r\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=(r\#\varepsilon(h_{(1)})1)\otimes h_{(2)}=(r\#1)\otimes h,

while at the right-hand side

(\operatorname{id}_{R}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}_{H})((r\#h)\otimes 1)=(r\#\varepsilon(h)1)\otimes 1=(r\#1)\otimes\varepsilon(h)1.

Comparing, we get
h=\varepsilon(h)1\in K
, and hence,
r\#h=r\varepsilon(h)\#1\in R\#_{\sigma}1\cong R
. Thus
A^{\operatorname{co}H}\cong R
, as desired.

Hopf crossed products are relevant for characterizing Hopf Galois extensions having the normal basis property (see Theorem
2.75
).

A particular case of Hopf crossed products is when the cocycle
\sigma
is
trivial
, that is, when
\sigma(g,h)=\varepsilon(g)\varepsilon(h)
for all
g,h\in H
. In this case, we write
R\#H
and the multiplication defined in (
13
) is simply
(r\#g)(s\#h)=r(g_{(1)}\cdot s)\#g_{(2)}h
, for all
r,s\in R
and
g,h\in H
.

R\#H
is called the
smash product
of
R
and
H
.

Under these conditions, Theorem
2.49
can be restated as follows.

Theorem 2.51
.

Let
R
be an
H
-module algebra. Then
A:=R\#H
is a
H
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho:A\rightarrow A\otimes H
given by

\rho(r\#h)=(r\#h_{(1)})\otimes h_{(2)},\qquad\mbox{for all }r\in R\mbox{ and }h\in H.

Moreover,
A^{\operatorname{co}H}\cong R
.

Corollary 2.52

(e.g.
[
18
, Example 6.4.3]
)
.

Let
R
be an
H
-module algebra. Then
R\subset R\#H
is an
H
-Galois extension.

Proof 2.53
.

We have
\beta:(R\#H)\otimes_{R}(R\#H)\rightarrow(R\#H)\otimes H
defined as
\beta(z\otimes w)=(z\otimes 1)\rho(w)
, for all
z,w\in R\#H
. But since the first tensor product is taken over
R\#1\cong R
, for all
r,s\in A
and
h,g\in H
we have
(r\#h)(s\#1)\otimes(1\#g)=(r\#h)\otimes(s\#g)
. Thus, it is enough to study
\beta
on elements of the form
(r\#h)\otimes(1\#g)
. That is,

\displaystyle\beta((r\#h)\otimes(1\#g))

\displaystyle=((r\#h)\otimes 1)\rho(1\#g)=((r\#h)\otimes 1)\left((1\#g_{(1)})\otimes g_{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=((r\#h)(1\#g_{(1)}))\otimes g_{(2)}=(r(h_{(1)}\cdot 1)\#h_{(2)}g_{(1)})\otimes g_{(2)}

\displaystyle=(r\varepsilon(h_{(1)})\#h_{(2)}g_{(1)})\otimes g_{(2)}=(r\#hg_{(1)})\otimes g_{(2)}.

As in the proof of Proposition
2.37
,
\beta^{-1}:(R\#H)\otimes H\rightarrow(R\#H)\otimes_{R}(R\#H)
is given by

\beta^{-1}((r\#h)\otimes g)=(r\#hS(g_{(1)}))\otimes(1\#g_{(2)}),\qquad\mbox{for all }r\in R\mbox{ and }h,g\in H.

Indeed, if
r\in R
and
h,g\in H
, we have

\displaystyle\beta^{-1}(\beta((r\#h)\otimes(1\#g)))

\displaystyle=\beta^{-1}\left((r\#hg_{(1)})\otimes g_{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=(r\#hg_{(1)}S((g_{(2)})_{(1)}))\otimes(1\#(g_{(2)})_{(2)})

\displaystyle=(r\#hg_{(1)}S(g_{(2)}))\otimes(1\#g_{(3)})=(r\#h\varepsilon(g_{(1)}))\otimes(1\#g_{(2)})

\displaystyle=(r\#h)\otimes(1\#g),

\displaystyle\beta(\beta^{-1}((r\#h)\otimes g))

\displaystyle=\beta\left((r\#hS(g_{(1)}))\otimes(1\#g_{(2)})\right)

\displaystyle=(r\#hS(g_{(1)})(g_{(2)})_{(1)})\otimes(g_{(2)})_{(2)}

\displaystyle=(r\#hS(g_{(1)})g_{(2)})\otimes g_{(3)}=(r\#h\varepsilon(g_{(1)}))\otimes g_{(2)}

\displaystyle=(r\#h)\otimes g.

Therefore,
R\subset R\#H
is a
H
-Galois extension.

Smash products are relevant for Hopf Galois extensions since, when
H
is finite dimensional, a generator of
\operatorname{Mod}_{H}
describes all
H^{*}
-Galois extensions (see Theorem
2.80
).

2.7.7

Groups acting on sets

The Galois map
\beta
can be seen as the dual of a natural map arising whenever a group acts on a set, as this example shows. Recall that if
G
is a (multiplicative) group and
X
is a non-empty set, a function
\mu:X\times G\rightarrow X
is called a
(right) group action of
G
on
X
, which we denote by
(x,g)\mapsto x\cdot g
, if

x\cdot 1=x\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad x\cdot(gh)=(x\cdot g)\cdot h,\qquad\mbox{for all }x\in X\mbox{ and }g,h\in G.

The action is said to be
free
if for a given
g\in G
such that
x\cdot g=x
, for some
x\in X
, it follows that
g=1
; in other words, no element in
G
, besides 1, has fixed points. It is not hard to check that an action is free if and only if, given
g,h\in G
, the existence of an
x\in X
such that
x\cdot g=x\cdot h
implies
g=h
.

Also, recall that for any
x\in X
, its
orbit
is defined by
x\cdot G:=\{x\cdot g:g\in G\}
. The set of all orbits of
X
under the action of
G
is denoted by
X/G
and is called the
quotient
of the action.

Consider the map
\alpha:X\times G\rightarrow X\times X
given by
(x,g)\mapsto(x,x\cdot g)
. Notice that
\alpha
is injective if only if the action is free. Moreover, the image of this map can bee seen as a pull-back. Indeed,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in X\times X:y=x\cdot g\mbox{ for some }g\in G\}

\displaystyle=\{(x,y)\in X\times X:x\cdot G=y\cdot G\}

\displaystyle=X\times_{X/G}X,

called
the fiber product
of
X
with itself over
X/G
via the canonical map
x\mapsto x\cdot G
.

We want to dualize this scenario; for simplicity assume that
X
and
G
are finite. We denote by
A:=^{X}
the algebra of functions from
X
to endowed with the pointwise addition and multiplication. The unit of this algebra is the map
1_{A}:X\rightarrow
given by
x\mapsto 1
. We say that
a\in A
is
constant on
G
-orbits
if
a(x\cdot g)=a(x)
, for all
x\in X
and
g\in G
. The set of functions constant on
G
-orbits is denoted by
X/G
. Finally, recall that
H:=(G)^{*}=^{G}
