\displaystyle=h^{(1)}{h^{(2)}}^{(3)}{h^{(2)}}^{(2)}{h^{(2)}}^{(1)}h^{(3)}=h_{(1)}S(h_{(2)})S^{2}(h_{(3)})S(h_{(4)})h_{(5)}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(h_{(1)})S(S(h_{(2)}))\varepsilon(h_{(3)})=S^{2}(\varepsilon(h_{(1)})h_{(2)})\varepsilon(h_{(3)})

\displaystyle=S^{2}(h_{1})\varepsilon(h_{(2)})=S^{2}(h).

Thus,
\theta=S^{2}
.

Example 2.100

(Simple algebraic field extensions)
.

Recall that a simple field extension
E
of a field is one obtained by the adjunction of a single element, i.e.,
E=(\alpha)
. In this case
\alpha
is called primitive. It is known that if
\alpha
is algebraic over , then

E=(\alpha)=\left\{\dfrac{f(\alpha)}{g(\alpha)}:f,g\in[x],\,g(\alpha)\neq 0\right\}\cong[x]/\langle q_{\alpha}(x)\rangle,

where
[x]
is the classical univariate polynomial algebra over and
q_{\alpha}(x)
is the minimal polynomial of
\alpha
over , i.e., the unique monic -polynomial of smallest degree satisfied by
\alpha
(see e.g.
[
97
, Theorem 2.4.1]
). Moreover, if
d=\operatorname{dg}(q_{\alpha}(x))
, then the set
\{1,\alpha,\ldots,\alpha^{d-1}\}
is a -basis for
E
.

Suppose that
\subset E
is a Galois extension of fields. Then, if
G=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\}
is the associated Galois group and
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}\subset^{G}
is the dual basis, by Theorem
2.39
, we known that the Galois map
\beta:E\otimes E\rightarrow E\otimes^{G}
, given by

F\otimes G\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}Fg_{i}(G)\otimes p_{i},\qquad\mbox{for all }F,G\in E,

is bijective. Notice that the simple extension

E(\gamma)=(\alpha)(\gamma)=(\alpha,\gamma)=\left\{\dfrac{f(\alpha,\gamma)}{g(\alpha,\gamma)}:f,g\in[x,y],\,g(\alpha,\beta)\neq 0\right\}

can be identified with the algebra
E\otimes E
in such a way that
\alpha\mapsto\alpha\otimes 1
and
\gamma\mapsto 1\otimes\alpha
. Then, the inverse of
1_{E}\otimes p_{k}
by
\beta
is

P_{k}:=\prod_{j\neq k}\dfrac{\beta-g_{j}(\alpha)}{g_{k}(\alpha)-g_{j}(\alpha)},\qquad\mbox{for all }k=1,\ldots,n.

Indeed, having in mind the identification, for all
1\leq k\leq n
we have

\displaystyle\beta(P_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j\neq k}\dfrac{1g_{i}(\alpha)-g_{j}(\alpha)g_{i}(1)}{g_{k}(\alpha)g_{i}(1)-g_{j}(\alpha)g_{i}(1)}\otimes p_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j\neq k}\dfrac{g_{i}(\alpha)-g_{j}(\alpha)}{g_{k}(\alpha)-g_{j}(\alpha)}\otimes p_{i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\neq k}\dfrac{g_{k}(\alpha)-g_{j}(\alpha)}{g_{k}(\alpha)-g_{j}(\alpha)}\otimes p_{k}=1_{E}\otimes p_{k}.

Hence, following the proof of Theorem
2.96
, we know that the map
\mu:E\rightarrow E\otimes E\otimes E
, given by

F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(F)\otimes P_{i},\qquad\mbox{for all }F\in E,

makes
E
into a -torsor.

Example 2.101

(Noncommutative quantum torsor with no character)
.

For a fixed non-negative integer
n
, suppose that the field contains a
n
-th primitive root of unity
q\neq 1
. For any
\alpha,\beta\in^{\times}
, the -algebra generated by the elements
x
and
y
together with the relations
x^{n}=\alpha
,
y^{n}=\beta
, and
xy=qyx
, is called the
noncommutative algebra without character
A_{\alpha,\beta}^{(n)}
. It is known that this algebra is a non-trivial cyclic algebra and
\dim_{A}{}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(n)}=n^{2}
. If
n=2
, it is an algebra of quaternions (cf. Grunspan, 2003, Example 2.8).

Taking
T=A_{\alpha,\beta}^{(n)}
, we define
\mu:T\rightarrow T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T
as

\mu(x)=x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mu(y)=y\otimes y^{-1}\otimes y.

Then

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\mu)\mu(x)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\mu)(x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x)=x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x

\displaystyle=(\mu\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})(x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x)=(\mu\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})\mu(x),

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes m)\mu(x)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes m)(x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x)=x\otimes 1,

\displaystyle(m\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})\mu(x)

\displaystyle=(m\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})(x\otimes x^{-1}\otimes x)=1\otimes x.

The same calculations are valid for
y
, so
T=A_{\alpha,\beta}^{(n)}
together with
\mu
is a quantum -torsor.

2.9.2

More on quantum torsors

Now we discuss some further results on quantum torsors. For instance, Grunspan found that if the base ring is a field then we can attach two Hopf algebras to any quantum torsor.

Theorem 2.102

(Reconstruction Theorem,
[
4
, Theorem 2.10]
)
.

Let
T
be a quantum -torsor with associated map
\mu:T\rightarrow T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T
and Grunspan map
\theta:T\rightarrow T
. Take

H_{l}(T):=\{z\in T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}:(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\theta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}})(\mu\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}})(z)=(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes\mu^{\operatorname{op}})(z)\}.

(41)

Then, the following assertions hold:

(i)

If
z\in H_{l}(T)
, then both
m_{T}(z)
and
m_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}(\theta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}})(z)
are equal to a common scalar denoted by
\varepsilon(z)1_{T}
.

(ii)

If
z\in H_{l}(T)
, then
\Delta(z):=(\mu\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}})(z)\in H_{l}(T)\otimes H_{l}(T)
.

(iii)

By defining
m_{H_{l}(T)}
as the restriction of
m_{T}\otimes m_{T}^{\operatorname{op}}
to
H_{l}(T)
and
u_{H_{l}(T)}:\rightarrow H_{l}(T)
as
u_{H_{l}(T)}(1)=1_{T}\otimes 1_{T}
,
H_{l}(T)
becomes a bialgebra.

(iv)

\operatorname{Im}(u_{T})\subset H_{l}(T)\otimes T
and
\gamma_{T}:=\mu:T\rightarrow H_{l}(T)\otimes T
embeds
T
with a left
H_{l}(T)
-comodule structure.

Moreover, if we set
S_{H_{l}(T)}(z):=\tau_{T}(\theta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}})(z)
, for all
z\in H_{l}(T)
, then we obtain
\operatorname{Im}(S_{H_{l}(T)})\subset H_{l}(T)
and hence
H_{l}(T)
is a Hopf algebra.

In fact, the result is also valid for
[[x]]
-torsors, providing
T
is topologically free over
[[x]]

[
4
]
.

Similarly, for every -torsor
T
, we can define a Hopf algebra structure on the set

H_{r}(T)=\{z\in T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T:(\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\theta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})(\operatorname{id}_{T^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\mu)(z)=(\mu^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})(z)\}.

(42)

We also have
\operatorname{Im}(\mu)\subset T\otimes H_{r}(T)
and hence the map
\delta_{T}:=\mu=T\rightarrow T\otimes H_{r}(T)
equips
T
with a right
H_{r}(T)
-comodule algebra structure. Moreover, relation
20
implies that the two structures of left
H_{l}(T)
-comodule algebra and right
H_{r}(T)
-comodule algebra are compatible. Therefore, we get the following result.

Corollary 2.103

(
[
4
, Corollary 4.13]
)
.

Let
T
be a quantum -torsor. Then
T
is a
(H_{l}(T),H_{r}(T))
-bicomodule algebra of .

Grunspan proved that, in fact,
T
is a
(H_{l}(T),H_{r}(T))
-biGalois object, while also providing properties of
H_{l}(T)
and
H_{r}(T)
and their explicit calculation for examples.

Roughly speaking, the next result establishes that these Hopf algebras attached to quantum torsors and the ones found in Hopf biGalois extensions (see e.g.
[
23
]
) are the same. Since the proof uses either Hopf biGalois theory, or Miyashita–Ulbrich techniques, and those topics are not covered in this document, we shall omit it (see
[
96
, Section 4]
).

Proposition 2.104

(
[
96
, Proposition 3.4]
)
.

(i)

Let
T
be a faithfully flat
H
-Galois object and consider the quantum torsor structure associated in Theorem
2.96
. Then
H_{r}(T)\cong H
and
H_{l}(T)=(T\otimes T)^{\operatorname{co}H}
.

(ii)

If
T
is a quantum torsor, then the quantum torsor associated in Theorem
2.96
to the
H_{r}(T)
-Galois object
T
coincides with
T
.

We end this section by discussing a generalization of quantum torsors proposed by Schauenburg
[
24
]
. For that, let
B
be a
K
-algebra and
B\subset T
an algebra extension such that
T
is a faithfully flat
K
-module. Since
T\otimes_{B}T
is obviously a
(B,B)
-bimodule, we can consider the
centralizer

C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T):=\{t\in T\otimes_{B}T:b\cdot t=t\cdot b,\,\mbox{for all }b\in B\}.

This module is endowed with an algebra structure.

Lemma 2.105
.

The centralizer
C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
is a
K
-algebra with multiplication given by
(x\otimes y)(z\otimes w):=zx\otimes yw
, for all
x\otimes y
and
z\otimes w\in C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
, and unit
1_{T}\otimes 1_{T}
.

Proof 2.106
.

If
x\otimes y,z\otimes w\in C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
, then

\displaystyle b[(x\otimes y)(z\otimes w)]

\displaystyle=b(zx\otimes yw)=bzx\otimes yw=(x\otimes y)(bz\otimes w)=(x\otimes y)[b(x\otimes w)]

\displaystyle=(x\otimes y)[(z\otimes w)b]=(x\otimes y)(z\otimes wb)=zx\otimes ywb=(zx\otimes yw)b

\displaystyle=[(x\otimes y)(z\otimes w)]b.

Thus,
C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
is indeed closed under this operation. The other properties are immediate to check.

Definition 2.107

(Generalized quantum torsor)
.

Let
B
be a
K
-algebra and
B\subset T
an algebra extension such that
T
is a faithfully flat
K
-module. A
generalized quantum
B
-torsor
structure on
T
is a map
\mu:T\rightarrow T\otimes C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
such that if the induced map
\mu_{0}:T\rightarrow T\otimes T\otimes_{B}T
is denoted by
\mu_{0}(x):=x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}
for any
x\in T
, then the following relations hold:

\displaystyle x^{(1)}x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\ 1\otimes x\in T\otimes_{B}T,

\displaystyle x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}x^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\ x\otimes 1\in T\otimes T,

\displaystyle\mu(b)=

\displaystyle\ b\otimes 1\otimes 1,\,\forall b\in B,

(43)

\displaystyle\mu(x^{(1)})\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\ x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes\mu(x^{(3)})\in T\otimes T\otimes_{B}T\otimes_{B}T.

(44)

Note that (
43
) implies that
\mu
is a left
B
-module map and hence the relation (
44
) actually makes sense. This generalization of quantum torsor also induces a descend datum.

Lemma 2.108

(
[
24
, Lemma 2.8.3]
)
.

Let
T
be a generalized quantum
B
-torsor with associated map
\mu
. Then
D(x\otimes y)=xy^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}\otimes y^{(3)}
defines a
T/K
-descent datum on
T\otimes_{B}T
. Moreover, it satisfies
(T\otimes D)\mu(x)=x^{(1)}\otimes 1\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}
, and
D(T\otimes_{B}T)\subset T\otimes C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
.

Proof 2.109
.

The calculations are completely similar to those made for Lemma
2.88
. The only new part is the last inclusion, which is immediately obtained from noticing that, by definition,
y^{(2)}\otimes y^{(3)}
is in the centralizer.

Recall that for an arbitrary descent datum
D
on
\operatorname{DD}(S/R)
over
M
, Lemma
2.87
gives an associated
R
-module
{}^{D}M=\{m\in M:D(m)=1\otimes m\}
. From the above, we conclude that in our setup
{}^{D}(T\otimes_{B}T)\subset C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
. Hence, the following result generalizes Theorem
2.90
.

Theorem 2.110

(
[
24
, Theorem 2.8.4]
)
.

Let
T
be a generalized quantum
B
-torsor with associated map
\mu
such that
T
is faithfully flat as right
B
-module. If

H:={}^{D}(T\otimes_{B}T)=\{x\otimes y\in T\otimes_{B}T:xy^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}\otimes y^{(3)}=1\otimes x\otimes y\},

then the following assertions hold:

(i)

H
is a
K
-flat Hopf algebra. The algebra structure is that of a subalgebra of the algebra
C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
; comultiplication and counit are given by

\Delta(x\otimes y)=x\otimes y^{(1)}\otimes y^{(2)}\otimes y^{(3)}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon(x\otimes y)=xy.

(ii)

T
is a right
H
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho:=\mu
. Moreover,
T^{\operatorname{co}H}=B
.

(iii)

B\subset T
is a right
H
-Galois extension.

Proof 2.111
.

Again, the calculations are not essentially different from the ones for Theorem
2.90
, the only difference being that, in this case, the assumption of faithful flatness of
T_{B}
is used to deduce, along the bijectivity of the Galois map
\beta:T\otimes_{B}T\rightarrow T\otimes H
, that
H
is a faithful flat
K
-module.

Finally, we have the following result which, together with the previous one, establishes the equivalence

\mbox{Generalized quantum torsors}\Leftrightarrow\mbox{Hopf Galois extensions},

provided conditions of faithful flatness.

Theorem 2.112

(
[
24
, Lemma 2.8.5]
)
.

Let
H
be a faithfully flat
K
-Hopf algebra and
T^{\operatorname{co}H}\subset T
a faithfully flat right
H
-Galois extension. If
B:=T^{\operatorname{co}H}
, then
T
is a generalized quantum
B
-torsor with associated map
\mu:T\rightarrow T\otimes C_{B}(T\otimes_{B}T)
defined by

\mu(x)=x_{(0)}\otimes{x_{(1)}}^{[1]}\otimes{x_{(1)}}^{[2]},\qquad\mbox{for all }x\in T,

(45)

where
h^{[1]}\otimes h^{[2]}:=\beta^{-1}(1\otimes h)\in T\otimes_{B}T
, whit
\beta:T\otimes_{B}T\rightarrow T\otimes H
the Galois map.

2.10

Hopf Galois systems

Almost parallel to the development of quantum torsors, Bichon presented another formulation for noncommutative torsors
[
6
]
. That approach was made by noticing that a classical torsor naturally gives rise to a grupoid with two objects. Although the axiomatisation looks slightly complicated, it is also more natural an easier to handle.

Definition 2.113

(Hopf Galois system)
.

A
K
-Hopf Galois system
consists of four
K
-algebras
(A,B,Z,T)
satisfying the following axioms:

(HGS1)

A
and
B
are
K
-bialgebras,

(HGS2)

Z
is an
(A,B)
-bicomodule algebra with respective structure maps
\alpha_{Z}:Z\rightarrow A\otimes Z
and
\beta_{Z}:Z\rightarrow Z\otimes B
.

(HGS3)

There exist algebra morphisms
\gamma:A\rightarrow Z\otimes T
and
\delta:B\rightarrow T\otimes Z
such that the following relations hold:

\displaystyle(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})\alpha_{Z}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\delta)\beta_{Z},

(46)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\gamma)\Delta_{A}

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T})\gamma,

(47)

\displaystyle(\delta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{B})\Delta_{B}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes\beta_{Z})\delta.

(48)

(HGS4)

There exists a
K
-linear map
S:T\rightarrow Z
such that the following relations hold:

\displaystyle m_{Z}(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S)\gamma

\displaystyle=u_{Z}\varepsilon_{A},

(49)

\displaystyle m_{Z}(S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})\delta

\displaystyle=u_{Z}\varepsilon_{B}.

(50)

We may extend Heyneman–Sweedler notation to Hopf Galois systems by writing

\gamma(a)=a_{Z}\otimes a_{T}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta(b)=b_{T}\otimes b_{Z},\qquad\mbox{for all }a\in A\mbox{ and }b\in B.

It can be proved that in any Hopf Galois system
(A,B,Z,T)
the bialgebras
A
and
B
are in fact Hopf algebras, and
S:T\rightarrow Z^{\operatorname{op}}
is an algebra morphism
[
6
, Corollaries 1.3 and 1.10]
.

The following result relates Hopf Galois systems with Hopf biGalois objects.

Theorem 2.114

(
[
6
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
(A,B,Z,T)
be a
K
-Hopf Galois system with
Z
faithfully flat over
K
. Then
Z
is an
(A,B)
-biGalois object.

Proof 2.115
.

First, we have to prove that the composition

\beta_{l}:\hbox to257.03pt{\vbox to17.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 128.51474pt\lower-7.49304pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\eta_{l}:A\otimes Z\rightarrow Z\otimes Z
be the map defined as the composition

\eta_{l}:\hbox to374.22pt{\vbox to17.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 187.10999pt\lower-7.49304pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

For all
a\in A
and
z,w\in Z
we have the equalities

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes m_{Z})(a\otimes z\otimes w)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(a\otimes zw)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(a_{Z}\otimes a_{T}\otimes zw)=a_{Z}\otimes S(a_{T})\otimes zw

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(a_{Z}\otimes S(a_{T})\otimes z\otimes w)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(a_{Z}\otimes a_{T}\otimes z\otimes w)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(a\otimes z\otimes w),

so,

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes m_{Z})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}).

(51)

Hence,

\displaystyle\eta_{l}\beta_{l}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes m_{Z})(\alpha_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e105}}}{=}(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\delta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\beta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e104}}}{=}(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes m_{Z})(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes u_{Z}\varepsilon_{B}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\beta_{Z}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})

\displaystyle\overset{\mbox{\eqref{e32}}}{=}\operatorname{id}_{Z\otimes Z}.

and similarly to the first part, one can show that
\eta_{r}
is the inverse of
\beta_{r}
. Finally, since
Z
is
K
-faithfully flat, by Proposition
2.35
, it is
(A,B)
-faithfully flat.

The converse is proven using techniques of Tannaka duality (see
[
98
]
and
[
6
, Remark 1.9]
) that are not covered in this document.

Theorem 2.116

(
[
6
, Corollary 1.8]
and
[
16
, Corollary 1]
)
.

Let
A
be a faithfully flat
K
-Hopf algebra and
Z
a faithfully flat left
A
-Galois object. Then there exists a Hopf algebra
B
and an algebra
T
such that
(A,B,Z,T)
is a Hopf Galois system.

Hence with certain assumptions of faithful flatness, if we glue together Theorem
2.90
(quantum torsors
\Rightarrow
Galois objects), Theorem
2.96
(Galois objects
\Rightarrow
quantum torsors), Theorem
2.114
(Hopf Galois systems
\Rightarrow
(bi)Galois objects) and Theorem
2.116
(Galois objects
\Rightarrow
Hopf Galois systems), we have the following equivalences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mbox{Hopf Galois}\\
\mbox{systems}\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mbox{Hopf Galois}\\
\mbox{objects}\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mbox{Quantum}\\
\mbox{torsors}\end{matrix}}

(52)

Clearly, this means that every Hopf Galois system gives rise to a quantum torsor and vice versa. However, for the sake of completeness, and because some interesting interactions arise, we will study the explicit equivalence between quantum torsors and Hopf Galois systems in Section
2.10.2
. Also, notice that we are not saying that the correspondences of (
52
) are necessarily bijective or functorial.

2.10.1

Examples of Hopf Galois systems

In this section we address some examples of Hopf Galois systems, which are adapted from
[
6
,
23
]
. As with quantum torsors, the novelty of this new approach is the inclusion of new examples.

Example 2.117

(Hopf algebras)
.

Let
H
be a
K
-Hopf algebra. If we put
A=B=Z=T=H
,
\alpha_{Z}=\beta_{Z}=\gamma=\delta=\Delta_{H}
and
S=S_{H}
, then
(A,B,Z,T)
is a Hopf Galois system. Indeed, (
46
)-(
48
) correspond to the coassociativity and (
49
)-(
50
) to the main property of the antipode.

Example 2.118

(Hopf algebras twisted by
2
-cocycles)
.

Let
H
be a faithfully flat
K
-Hopf algebra. Using the universal property of the tensor product, a given
\sigma\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(H\otimes H,K)
corresponds to an unique bilinear
K
-form, so we shall write
\sigma(h\otimes k)=\sigma(h,k)
, for all
h,k\in H
. Since
H\otimes H
is a coalgebra and
K
is an algebra,
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(H\otimes H,K)
is also an algebra with the convolution product. We say that
\sigma:H\otimes H\rightarrow K
is a
2
-cocycle
if
\sigma
is a convolution invertible
K
-linear map satisfying

\sigma(g_{(1)},h_{(1)})\sigma(g_{(2)}h_{(2)},k)=\sigma(h_{(1)},k_{(1)})\sigma(g,h_{(2)}k_{(2)})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sigma(h,1)=\sigma(1,h)=\varepsilon(h)1,

for all
g,h,k\in H
. By
[
99
, Theorem 1.6]
, if
\overline{\sigma}
denotes the convolution inverse of
\sigma
, then it satisfies

\overline{\sigma}(f_{(1)}g_{(1)},h)\overline{\sigma}(f_{(2)},g_{(2)})=\overline{\sigma}(f,g_{(1)}h_{(1)})\overline{\sigma}(g_{(2)}h_{(2)})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{\sigma}(h,1)=\overline{\sigma}(1,h)=\varepsilon(h)1,

for all
f,g,h\in H
.

For a fixed
2
-cocycle
\sigma
over
H
, we consider a new multiplication over the
K
-module
H
, given by

h\cdot_{\sigma}k:=\sigma(h_{(1)},k_{(1)})h_{(2)}k_{(2)},\qquad\mbox{for all }h,k\in H.

This new algebra is denoted by
{}_{\sigma}{H}
. Similarly, another possible product for
H
is

h\cdot_{\overline{\sigma}}k:=\overline{\sigma}(h_{(2)},k_{(2)})h_{(1)}k_{(1)},\qquad\mbox{for all }h,k\in H,

and the induced algebra is denoted by
H_{\overline{\sigma}}
. Notice that
H_{\overline{\sigma}}
is a
H
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho_{l}=\Delta_{H}
.

Finally, we can also define a new Hopf algebra
{}_{\sigma}{H}_{\overline{\sigma}}
, which is isomorphic to
H
as coalgebra, with multiplication

k\cdot h:=\sigma(h_{(1)},k_{(1)})\overline{\sigma}(h_{(3)},k_{(3)})h_{(2)}k_{(2)},\qquad\mbox{for all }h,k\in H,

and antipode
S^{\sigma}(h):=\sigma(h_{(1)},S(h_{(2)}))\overline{\sigma}(S(h_{(4)}),h_{(5)})S(h_{(3)})
. It can be shown that
{H}_{\overline{\sigma}}
is a right
{}_{\sigma}{H}_{\overline{\sigma}}
-comodule algebra with structure map
\rho_{r}=\Delta
, and that it is a
(H,{}_{\sigma}{H}_{\overline{\sigma}})
-bicomodule algebra. The details of these constructions can be found in
[
99
, Section 2]
and
[
23
, Section 3]
. From
[
6
, Proposition 2.1]
and
[
63
, Theorem 1.6.(a5)]
we know that
(H,{}_{\sigma}{H}_{\overline{\sigma}},{H}_{\overline{\sigma}},{}_{\sigma}{H})
is a Hopf Galois system.

Example 2.119

(Hopf algebras of a non-degenerate bilinear form)
.

Let be an algebraically closed field and
n,m>1
. For two fixed invertible matrices
E_{m\times m}
and
F_{n\times n}
, we denote by
\mathcal{B}(E,F)
the -algebra generated by
\{x_{ij}:1\leq i\leq m,\,1\leq j\leq n\}
together with the relations
F^{-1t}XEX=I_{n}
and
XF^{-1t}XE=I_{m}
, where
X
is the matrix
(x_{ij})
and
I_{n}
and
I_{m}
are the identity matrices of size
n
and
m
, respectively. For the particular case
n=m
and
E=F
we simply write
\mathcal{B}(E)
. This (Hopf) algebra was introduced by Dubois-Violette and Launer
[
100
]
, and it turns out to be the function algebra on the quantum (symmetry) group of a non-degenerate bilinear form
[
101
, Section 2]
. For any matrix
A=(a_{ij})
over
\mathcal{B}(E)
, the comultiplication, counit and antipode are given by

\Delta(a_{ij})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{ik}\otimes a_{ik},\qquad\varepsilon(a_{ij})=\delta_{ij}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S(A)=E^{-1t}AE,

where
\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker delta.

If
\operatorname{tr}(E^{t}E^{-1})=\operatorname{tr}(F^{t}F^{-1})
, Bichon proved that

(\mathcal{B}(E),\mathcal{B}(F),\mathcal{B}(E,F),\mathcal{B}(F,E))

is a Hopf Galois system
[
6
, Proposition 3.1]
. Even without the assumption on the traces it can be shown that
\mathcal{B}(E,F)
is a
(\mathcal{B}(E),\mathcal{B}(F))
-biGalois object
[
101
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Example 2.120

(Free Hopf algebras generated by dual matrix coalgebras)
.

Let
C
be a
K
-coalgebra. We say that a
K
-Hopf algebra
H(C)
is a
free Hopf algebra generated by
C
if there exists a coalgebra map
i:C\rightarrow H(C)
such that the following universal property is satisfied: for any
K
-Hopf algebra
H
and any coalgebra morphism
f:C\rightarrow H
there exists an unique Hopf algebra morphism
\overline{f}:H(C)\rightarrow H
such that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

From the above, it follows that
H(C)
is unique up to isomorphism. The existence of such free Hopf algebra is explicitly shown in
[
102
, Section 1]
by constructing
H(C)
as follows. Let
\{V_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
be the sequence of coalgebras
V_{0}:=C
and
V_{i+1}:=V_{i}^{\operatorname{op}}
, for
i\geq 0
. We define
V:=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}V_{i}
, which is also a coalgebra via the induced pointwise operations. Considering the tensor algebra
T(V)
, we have a coalgebra map
S:V\rightarrow V^{\operatorname{op}}
given by
(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots)\mapsto(0,x_{0},x_{1},\ldots)
, which induces a bialgebra map
S:T(V)\rightarrow T(V)^{\operatorname{op}}
. Now, let

I=\langle x_{(1)}S(x_{(2)})-\varepsilon(x)1,S(x_{(1)})x_{(2)}-\varepsilon(x)1:x\in V\rangle.

One can check that
I
is in fact a Hopf ideal of
T(V)
, and therefore
H(C):=T(V)/I
is a Hopf algebra with antipode induced by
S
.

A particular case of the above is when
C=(M_{n}())^{*}
, where
M_{n}()
denotes the algebra of
n\times n
matrices over . In such case,
H(C)
is denoted by
H(n)
and corresponds to the -algebra generated by
\{u_{ij}^{(\alpha)}:1\leq i,j\leq n,\,\alpha\in\mathbb{N}\}
satisfying the relations

(u^{(\alpha)})^{t}u^{(\alpha+1)}=I_{n}=u^{(\alpha+1)}(u^{(\alpha)})^{t},

where
u^{(\alpha)}
is the
n\times n
matrix
(u_{ij}^{(\alpha)})

[
103
, Theorem 3.1]
. More generally, for
m,n\geq 1
we define
H(m,n)
as the algebra generated by
\{u_{ij}^{(\alpha)}:1\leq i\leq m,\,1\leq j\leq n,\,\alpha\in\mathbb{N}\}
together with the relations

(u^{(\alpha)})^{t}u^{(\alpha+1)}=I_{m}\quad\mbox{and}\quad u^{(\alpha+1)}t(u^{(\alpha)})=I_{n},

where
u^{(\alpha)}
is the
n\times m
matrix
(u_{ij}^{(\alpha)})
. For
m,n\geq 2
,
(H(m),H(n),H(m,n),H(n,m))
is a Hopf Galois system
[
6
, Proposition 5.2]
.

2.10.2

More on Hopf Galois systems

We explore further the connection between Hopf Galois systems and quantum torsors, by mentioning some results of Grunspan
[
4
,
16
]
. Recall that any quantum
K
-torsor
T
has an associated map
\mu:T\rightarrow T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T
, which is denoted by
\mu(x)=x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}
, and a Grunspan map
\theta:T\rightarrow T
satisfying

\theta(x)=x^{(1)}x^{(2)(3)}x^{(2)(2)}x^{(2)(1)}x^{(3)}.

Theorem 2.121

(
[
4
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Let
(A,B,Z,T)
be a
K
-Hopf Galois system. Then the map
\mu:Z\rightarrow Z\otimes Z^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes Z
given by

\mu=(\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes S\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})(\gamma\otimes\operatorname{id}_{Z})\alpha_{Z}
makes
Z
into a quantum
K
-torsor.

To see the converse of Theorem
2.121
, recall the construction of the Hopf algebra
H_{l}(T)
(resp.
H_{r}(T)
) defined in (
41
) (resp. (
42
)) as certain subalgebra of
T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}
(resp.
T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T
). Summarizing Theorem
2.102
, we have that the simple elements of
H_{l}(T)
are of the form
x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\in T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}
satisfying

x_{i}^{(1)}\otimes x_{i}^{(2)}\otimes\theta(x_{i}^{(3)})\otimes y_{i}=x_{i}\otimes y_{i}^{(3)}\otimes y_{i}^{(2)}\otimes y_{i}^{(1)}.

Moreover, the comultiplication, counit and antipode on
H_{l}(T)
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{H_{l}(T)}(x_{i}\otimes y_{i})=

\displaystyle\ x_{i}^{(1)}\otimes x_{i}^{(2)}\otimes x_{i}^{(3)}\otimes y_{i},

\displaystyle u_{T}\varepsilon_{H_{l}(T)}(x_{i}\otimes y_{i})=

\displaystyle\ x_{i}y_{i},

\displaystyle S_{H_{l}(T)}(x_{i}\otimes y_{i})=

\displaystyle\ y_{i}\otimes\theta(x_{i}).

Similarly, the elements of
H_{r}(T)
are of the form
x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\in T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T
satisfying

x_{i}\otimes\theta(y_{i}^{(1)})\otimes y_{i}^{(2)}\otimes y_{i}^{(3)}=x_{i}^{(3)}\otimes x_{i}^{(2)}\otimes x_{i}^{(1)}\otimes y_{i},

and the comultiplication, counit and antipode are given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{H_{r}(T)}(x_{i}\otimes y_{i})=

\displaystyle\ x_{i}\otimes y_{i}^{(1)}\otimes y_{i}^{(2)}\otimes y_{i}^{(3)},

\displaystyle u_{T}\varepsilon_{H_{r}(T)}(x_{i}\otimes y_{i})=

\displaystyle\ x_{i}y_{i},

\displaystyle S_{H_{r}(T)}(x_{i}\otimes y_{i})=

\displaystyle\ \theta(y_{i})\otimes x_{i}.

Theorem 2.122

(
[
16
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
T
be a faithfully flat quantum
K
-torsor with associated map
\mu
and Grunspan map
\theta
. Consider
A=H_{r}(T)
,
B=H_{l}(T)
, and
\rho_{r}:T\rightarrow T\otimes A
and
\rho_{l}:T\rightarrow B\otimes T
given by

\displaystyle\rho_{r}(x)=x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}\in T\otimes A\subset T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T,

\displaystyle\rho_{l}(x)=x^{(1)}\otimes x^{(2)}\otimes x^{(3)}\in B\otimes T\subset T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T,

for all
x\in T
, which define a structure of
(B,A)
-biGalois object on
T
. Then there is a natural structure of
K
-algebra on
Z:=(A\otimes T)^{\operatorname{co}A}=(T\otimes B)^{\operatorname{co}B}
as subalgebra of
T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}}
, so that
Z
becomes an
(A,B)
-biGalois object (in particular, an
(A,B)
-bicomodule algebra) via the morphisms
\alpha_{Z}=\Delta_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T}:Z\rightarrow A\otimes Z
and
\beta_{Z}=\operatorname{id}_{Z}\otimes\Delta_{B}:Z\rightarrow Z\otimes B
. Moreover, the algebra morphisms
\gamma:A\to Z\otimes T
and
\delta:B\to T\otimes Z
given by

\displaystyle\gamma(h)=x_{i}\otimes y_{i}^{(1)}\otimes y_{i}^{(2)}\otimes y_{i}^{(3)},\qquad\mbox{for }h=x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\in A\subset T^{\operatorname{op}}\otimes T,

\displaystyle\delta(h)=x_{i}^{(1)}\otimes x_{i}^{(2)}\otimes x_{i}^{(3)}\otimes y_{i},\qquad\mbox{for }h=x_{i}\otimes y_{i}\in B\subset T\otimes T^{\operatorname{op}},

and the
K
-linear map
S_{T}:T\rightarrow Z
given by

S_{T}(x)=[(\theta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{T}\otimes\theta)\mu^{\operatorname{op}}](x),\qquad\mbox{for all}x\in T,

make
(A,B,Z,T)
into a
K
-Hopf Galois system. Furthermore, the quantum torsor associated to this Hopf Galois system by Theorem
2.121
is isomorphic to
T
.

In fact, the Hopf Galois system obtained here satisfies some additional conditions of symmetry that make it
total
(see
[
16
, Definition 3.1]
).

Therefore, by adding Theorem
2.121
(Hopf Galois systems
\Rightarrow
quantum torsors) and Theorem
2.122
(quantum torsors
\Rightarrow
(total) Hopf Galois systems) to former results, we have explicitly closed diagram (
52
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mbox{Hopf Galois}\\
\mbox{systems}\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mbox{Hopf Galois}\\
\mbox{objects}\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\mbox{Quantum}\\
\mbox{torsors}\end{matrix}}

3

Families of noncommutative rings

In the last century, noncommutative rings and algebras have appeared in almost every subject of research, and not only mathematical contexts, but also in theoretical physics. Therefore, the study of certain algebras given by their generators and relations has become useful. However, within a more practical approach, some general families of noncommutative rings have been defined and studied along the years.

Although these collections do not cover, in general, every remarkable example, most of such families contain a notorious amount of distinguished algebras, even having the case that one object can be endowed with two or more different structures, as we shall discuss in the examples. In this section, we will address several of such families of rings, most of them having a polynomial behavior.

Popular for describing a broad number of algebras and for having a pioneering role in the systematic research on noncommutative rings, in Section
3.1
we address skew polynomial rings. Section
3.2
is dedicated to PBW extensions, which comprehend rings with the PBW basis property, while Section
3.3
reviews a generalization of that setup. Finally, in Section
3.4
we present a family of algebras that generalizes enveloping universal algebras of Lie algebras.

3.1

Skew polynomial rings

Introduced by Ore
[
7
]
, skew polynomial rings (also known as Ore extensions) are distinguished by their elements, which have a polynomial aspect but not necessarily the variable is assumed to commute with coefficients. For such ``commutation'' to take place, certain rule involving an endomorphism and a derivation of the ground ring is established.

Let
R
be a ring and
\sigma:R\rightarrow R
a ring endomorphism. An additive map
\delta:R\rightarrow R
is called a
\sigma
-derivation of
R
if
\delta(rs)=\sigma(r)\delta(s)+\delta(r)s
, for all
r,s\in R
. Notice that, in particular,
\delta(1)=\delta(1\cdot 1)=\sigma(1)\delta(1)+\delta(1)1=2\delta(1)
, whence
\delta(1)=0
.

Definition 3.1

(Skew polynomial ring)
.

Let
R
be a ring,
\sigma:R\rightarrow R
a ring endomorphism and
\delta:R\rightarrow R
a
\sigma
-derivation of
R
. A ring
A
such that

(O1)

A
contains
R
as a proper subring and
1_{R}=1_{A}
,

(O2)

There is a distinguishable element
x\in A
such that
A
is a left free
R
-module with basis
\{1,x,x^{2},x^{3},\ldots\}
,

(O3)

xr=\sigma(r)x+\delta(r)
, for all
r\in R
,

is called a
skew polynomial ring over
R
. In this case we write
A:=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
.

There are some constructive proofs showing the existence of skew polynomial rings, which verify the ring structure of
A=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
without falling in the tedious calculations of a direct proof
[
104
, Proposition 2.3]
. Moreover, such constructions guarantee that, given any ring
R
, any ring endomorphism
\sigma
of
R
and any
\sigma
-derivation of
R
, the skew polynomial ring
R[x;\sigma,\delta]
always exists.

Unless otherwise stated, for the remainder of the section we let
A:=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
be a skew polynomial ring over a fixed ring
R
. From
(O3)
it is natural to ask for a general formula that allows us to express
x^{i}r
(
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in R
) as a
polynomial
with
left coefficients
. Nevertheless, those calculations can be tricky. For instance, with
i=3
we end up with

x^{3}r=\sigma^{3}(r)x^{3}+[\delta\sigma^{2}(r)+\sigma\delta\sigma(r)+\sigma^{2}\delta(r)]x^{2}+[\delta^{2}\sigma(r)+\delta\sigma\delta(r)+\sigma\delta^{2}(r)]x+\delta^{3}(r).

However, using an inductive argument, it can be shown that the
multiplication rule
can be written as follows. Given
r\in R
and
i,k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
W[\delta^{k}\sigma^{i-k}](r)
the evaluation of
r
in the function given by the sum of all possible
words
that can be constructed with the alphabet formed by
k
-times the symbol
\delta
and
(i-k)
-times the symbol
\sigma
, where the concatenation is understood as the composition of functions. For instance, if
i=5
and
k=2
we get

\displaystyle W[\delta^{2}\sigma^{3}](r)

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\sigma^{3}(r)+\delta\sigma\delta\sigma^{2}(r)+\delta\sigma^{2}\delta\sigma(r)+\delta\sigma^{3}\delta(r)+\sigma\delta^{2}\sigma^{2}(r)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sigma\delta\sigma\delta\sigma(r)+\sigma\delta\sigma^{2}\delta(r)+\sigma^{2}\delta^{2}\sigma(r)+\sigma^{2}\delta\sigma\delta(r)+\sigma^{3}\delta^{2}(r).

In the general case, if
r\in R
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the following formula holds:

x^{i}r=\sum_{k=0}^{i}W[\delta^{k}\sigma^{i-k}](r)x^{i-k}.

(53)

Moreover, if
r,s\in R
and
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, then:

(rx^{i})(sx^{j})=r\sum_{k=0}^{i}W[\delta^{k}\sigma^{i-k}](s)x^{i+j-k}.

(54)

This discussion shows that in
A
the product of two
terms

rx^{n}
and
sx^{m}
is not necessarily another term, yet in general it will be a polynomial. However, by
(O2)
, it is quite clear that every element
p\in A
can be uniquely written as

p=\sum_{i=0}^{n}r_{i}x^{i}=r_{0}+r_{1}x+r_{2}x^{2}+\cdots+r_{n-1}x^{n-1}+r_{n}x^{n},\quad r_{i}\in R\mbox{ and }0\leq i\leq n.

The element
p
is usually denoted
p(x)
to emphasize the
indeterminate

x
. Following the classical terminology, the
r_{i}
are called the
coefficients
of
p(x)
. Hence, we conclude that the elements of
A
have a polynomial expression, which justifies the name given to this construction.

Definition 3.2
.

If
p(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}r_{i}x^{i}
is an element of
A
such that
r_{n}\neq 0
, we define:

(i)

\operatorname{dg}(p(x)):=n
as the
degree of
p(x)
,

(ii)

\operatorname{lc}(p(x)):=r_{n}
as the
leading coefficient
,

(iii)

\operatorname{lm}(p(x)):=x^{n}
as the
leading monomial
,

(iv)

\operatorname{lt}(p(x)):=\operatorname{lc}(p(x))\operatorname{lm}(p(x))=r_{n}x^{n}
as the
leading term
.

If all coefficient of
p(x)
are zero, we say that
p(x):=0
is the
zero polynomial
and in this case
\operatorname{lc}(0):=0
,
\operatorname{lm}(0):=0
and
\operatorname{lt}(0):=0
.

Lemma 3.3
.

If
p(x),q(x)\in A
and
p(x),q(x)\neq 0
, then

(i)

\operatorname{dg}(p(x))\geq 0
,

(ii)

\operatorname{dg}(p(x)+q(x))\leq\max\left\{\operatorname{dg}(p(x)),\operatorname{dg}(q(x))\right\}
,

(iii)

\operatorname{dg}(p(x)q(x))\leq\operatorname{dg}(p(x))+\operatorname{dg}(q(x))
.

Notice that no degree was defined for the zero polynomial. However, some authors put
\operatorname{dg}(0):=-\infty
, so (ii) and (iii) holds for every
p,q\in R[x;\sigma,\delta]
(see e.g.
[
104
, p. 37]
).

Since our work concerns algebras, the next result establishes when
A
has an algebra structure induced by the ring of coefficients
R
. We were not able to find a proof of it in the literature.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
R
be a
K
-algebra.
A=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
is a
K
-algebra having
R
as subalgebra if and only if
\sigma
and
\delta
are
K
-linear maps.

Proof 3.5
.

Suppose first that
A
is a
K
-algebra. Hence, for a given
k\in K
we must have
(k1_{R})x=x(k1_{R})
, but by
(O3)
,
x(k1_{R})=\sigma(k1_{R})x+\delta(k1_{R})
. Comparing and using
(O2)
we get
\sigma(k1_{R})=k1_{R}
and
\delta(k1_{R})=0
.

Conversely, if
\sigma
is a
K
-linear map and
\delta(k1_{R})=0
for every
k\in K
, we must guarantee a ring morphism
\phi:K\rightarrow A
such that
\operatorname{Im}(\phi)\subseteq Z(A)
. Since
1_{A}=1_{R}
and
R
is already a
K
-algebra, define
\phi(k)=k1_{R}
, for all
k\in K
. Obviously,
\phi
is a (unitary) ring morphism. Moreover, by
(O3)
,
x\phi(k)=\sigma(\phi(k))x+\delta(\phi(k))=\sigma(k1_{R})x+\delta(k1_{R})=(k1_{R})x=\phi(k)x
. Hence,
\operatorname{Im}(\phi)\subset Z(A)
.

For the remainder of this document, every time we have the hypothesis that
R
is a
K
-algebra, we will automatically assume that
\sigma
and
\delta
are
K
-linear, so
A
is also a
K
-algebra.

The following result is known as the universal property of skew polynomial rings.

Theorem 3.6

(e.g.
[
104
, Proposition 2.4]
)
.

Assume that
B
is a ring such that the following assertions hold:

(i)

There is a ring morphism
\phi:R\rightarrow B
,

(ii)

There is a distinguished element
y\in B
such that
y\phi(r)=\phi(\sigma(r))y+\phi(\delta(r))
for all
r\in R
.

Then there is a unique ring morphism
\psi:A\rightarrow B
such that
\psi(x)=y
and
\psi|_{R}=\phi
. The last relation can be represented by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

(55)

Here
\iota:R\rightarrow A
is the natural inclusion
\iota(r):=r
for all
r\in R
. Moreover, if
R
and
B
are
K
-algebras and
\phi
is a
K
-algebra morphism, then
\psi
is also a
K
-algebra morphism.

Proof 3.7
.

B
has a right
R
-module structure via
r\cdot b:=\phi(r)b
, for all
r\in R
and
b\in B
. On the other hand, by
(O2)
,
A
is a right free
R
-module with basis
\{x^{i}:i\geq 0\}
. Hence we can define a morphism of left
R
-modules
\psi:A\rightarrow B
via
\psi(x^{i})=y^{i}
, for all
i\geq 0
. Then
\psi
is given by

\displaystyle\psi(r_{0}+r_{1}x+\cdots+r_{n}x^{n})

\displaystyle=r_{0}\cdot\psi(1)+r_{1}\cdot\psi(x)+\cdots r_{n}\cdot\psi(x^{n})

\displaystyle=\phi(r_{0})+\phi(r_{1})y+\cdots+\phi(r_{n})y^{n}.

By definition, the diagram (
55
) is commutative. Furthermore,
\psi
preserves the unity element since
\psi(1)=\psi(1x^{0})=\phi(1)y^{0}=1
. We want
\psi
to be a ring morphism, so the only thing left to check is that

\psi(rx^{n}sx^{m})=\psi(rx^{n})\psi(sx^{m}),

(56)

for all
r,s\in R
and
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
. This is done by induction over
n
. The case
n=0
is trivial. If
n=1
, we have

\displaystyle\psi(rxsx^{m})

\displaystyle=\psi(r[\sigma(s)x+\delta(s)]x^{m})=\psi(r\sigma(s)x^{m+1}+r\delta(s)x^{m})

\displaystyle=\psi(r\sigma(s)x^{m+1})+\psi(r\delta(s)x^{m})=\phi(r\sigma(s))y^{m+1}+\phi(r\delta(s))y^{m}

\displaystyle=\phi(r)[\phi(\sigma(s))y+\phi(\delta(s))]y^{m}=\phi(r)y\phi(s)y^{m}

\displaystyle=\psi(rx)\psi(sx^{m}).

Assume now that (
56
) holds for a fixed
n
. Then

\displaystyle\psi(rx^{n+1}sx^{m})

\displaystyle=\psi(rx^{n}xsx^{m})=\psi(rx^{n}[\sigma(s)x+\delta(s)]x^{m})

\displaystyle=\psi(rx^{n}\sigma(s)x^{m+1})+\psi(rx^{n}\delta(s)x^{m})

\displaystyle=\psi(rx^{n})\psi(\sigma(s)x^{m+1})+\psi(rx^{n})\psi(\delta(s)x^{m})

\displaystyle=\psi(rx^{n})[\phi(\sigma(s))y+\phi(\delta(s))]y^{m}

\displaystyle=\psi(rx^{n})y\phi(s)y^{m}=\psi(rx^{n+1})\psi(sx^{m}).

Therefore
\psi
is a ring morphism. By construction,
\psi
is uniquely determined.

Finally, suppose that
R
and
B
are
K
-algebras, and that
\phi
is an algebra morphism. Then,
\psi(krx^{i})=\phi(kr)y^{i}=k\phi(r)y^{i}=k\psi(rx^{i})
, for all
r\in R
,
k\in K
and
i\geq 0
. This guarantees that
\psi
is
K
-linear and, since it is already a ring morphism, we have shown that it is an algebra map.

Corollary 3.8

(e.g.
[
104
, Corollary 2.5]
)
.

Assume that
B
is a ring such that the following assertions hold:

(i)

There is a ring morphism
\phi:R\rightarrow B
,

(ii)

There is a distinguished element
y\in B
such that
y\phi(r)=\phi(\sigma(r))y+\phi(\delta(r))
for all
r\in R
,

(iii)

B
satisfies the universal property of Theorem
3.6
.

Then there exists a ring isomorphism between
B
and
A
. Moreover, if
R
and
B
are
K
-algebras, then the isomorphism is an algebra map.

Proof 3.9
.

Since
A
satisfies the universal property and the condition (ii) of Theorem
3.6
holds for
B
, there is an uniquely ring morphism
\phi:A\rightarrow B
such that
\phi(x)=y
and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

is commutative. Similarly, since
B
satisfies the universal property and the relation

xr=\sigma(r)x+\delta(r)=\iota(\sigma(r))x+\iota(\delta(r)),\qquad\mbox{for all }r\in R,

holds in
A
, then there exists an uniquely ring morphism
\varphi:B\rightarrow A
such that
\varphi(y)=x
and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

is commutative. Moreover, using the respective universal properties of
A
and
B
with themselves, we get two additional commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{B}}

Since
\varphi\psi(x)=x
and
\varphi\psi\iota=\iota
, by uniqueness
\varphi\psi=\operatorname{id}_{A}
. Similarly,
\psi\varphi(y)=y
and
\psi\varphi\phi=\phi
, so
\psi\varphi=\operatorname{id}_{B}
. Hence, we conclude
B\cong A
as ring. The last claim is clear from the fact that the universal property lifts algebra maps.

Now we list some basic ring-theoretical properties of skew polynomial rings.

Proposition 3.10
.

Assume that
\sigma
is injective. If
R
is a domain, then
A
is also a domain.

Proof 3.11
.

Let
p(x)=p_{0}+p_{1}x+\cdots+p_{n}x^{n}\neq 0
and
q(x)=q_{0}+q_{1}x+\cdots+q_{m}x^{m}\neq 0
be two elements of
A
such that
p_{n},q_{m}\neq 0
. Then
\operatorname{lt}(pq)=p_{n}\sigma^{n}(q_{m})x^{n+m}\neq 0
, by the injectivity of
\sigma
. Hence,
pq\neq 0
.

It is clear that under these conditions,
\operatorname{gr}(pq)=\operatorname{gr}(p)+\operatorname{gr}(q)
for all
p,q\in A-\{0\}
. Moreover, the units of
A
coincide with the units of
R
.

Recall that
R
is said to be
left Noetherian
if any ascending chain of left ideals stabilizes. We mention a result of huge relevance which generalizes the well known Hilbert's Basis Theorem.

Theorem 3.12

(Hilbert's Basis Theorem for skew polynomial rings, e.g.
[
104
, Theorem 2.6]
)
.

If
R
is a left (resp. right) Noetherian ring and
\sigma
is bijective, then
A
is also a left (resp. right) Noetherian ring.

The construction of skew polynomial rings can be applied several times to obtain an
iterated skew polynomial ring
of the form
R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]
. For
1\leq i\leq n
, notice that
\sigma_{i}
and
\delta_{i}
must be defined as

\sigma_{i},\delta_{i}:R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{i-1};\sigma_{i-1},\delta_{i-1}]\longrightarrow R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{i-1};\sigma_{i-1},\delta_{i-1}].

For iterated skew polynomial rings an explicit basis over the original base ring is given.

Lemma 3.13
.

If
A=R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]
is an iterated skew polynomial ring over
R
, then the set
\operatorname{Mon}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):=\left\{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}:(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\right\}
is a left
R
-basis of
A
.

Proof 3.14
.

We proceed by induction over
n
, the number of variables. We denote

A_{i}:=R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{i};\sigma_{i},\delta_{i}],\qquad\mbox{for all }1\leq i\leq n.

Since for
n=1
the statement reduces to
(O2)
, there is nothing to prove.

Let
n=2
. Then, again by
(O2)
, the powers of
x_{2}
form an left basis of
A_{2}
over
A_{1}
, meaning that every element
p\in A_{2}
can be written as

p=p_{0}(x_{1})+p_{1}(x_{1})x_{2}+p_{2}(x_{1})x_{2}^{2}+\cdots+p_{m}(x_{1})x_{2}^{m},

with all
p_{j}(x_{1})\in A_{1}
,
0\leq j\leq m
. Since every
p_{j}(x_{1})
can be generated by powers of
x_{1}
, by distributivity, it is clear that
p
is generated by
\{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}:\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Now, suppose that
0=\sum_{i,j=0}^{n,m}r_{ij}x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j}
, for some
r_{ij}\in R
. By associativity,

\sum_{i,j=0}^{n,m}r_{ij}x_{1}^{i}x_{2}^{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n}r_{ij}x_{1}^{i}\right)x_{2}^{j},

so by linearly independence of the powers of
x_{2}
on
A
,
\sum_{i=0}^{n}r_{ij}x_{1}^{i}=0
, for every
1\leq j\leq m
. But now by the linearly independence of the powers of
x_{1}
on
A_{1}
, every
r_{ij}=0
.

Now, assume that
\operatorname{Mon}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})
is a left basis for
A_{n-1}
over
R
. As the previous case, every
p\in A
can be written as

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=p_{0}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})+p_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})x_{n}

\displaystyle\qquad+p_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})x_{n-1}^{2}+\cdots+p_{m}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1})x_{n-1}^{m},

with all
p_{j}\in A_{n-1}
,
0\leq j\leq m
. Using the induction hypothesis for every
p_{j}
, it is clear that
\operatorname{Mon}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
generates
p
. Now, suppose that

0=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq m_{i}\\
1\leq i\leq n\end{subarray}}r_{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}},\qquad\mbox{for some }r_{\alpha}\in R,\,\alpha:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}.

Associating,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq m_{i}\\
1\leq i\leq n\end{subarray}}r_{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}=\sum_{\alpha_{n}=0}^{n}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq m_{i}\\
1\leq i\leq n-1\end{subarray}}r_{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n-1}^{\alpha_{n-1}}\right)x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}.

Since the powers of
x_{n}
are a left basis for
A
over
A_{n-1}
, we must have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq m_{i}\\
1\leq i\leq n-1\end{subarray}}r_{\alpha}x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n-1}^{\alpha_{n-1}}=0,

but again by induction hypothesis, that only happens if and only if every
r_{\alpha}=0
, which shows the linearly independence of
\operatorname{Mon}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

We end this section with some examples, adapted from
[
104
,
9
]
, which illustrate that skew polynomial rings are indeed a generalization of more particular well-known cases.

Example 3.15

(Classical polynomial ring)
.

Take
\sigma=\operatorname{id}_{R}
and
\delta=0
. Therefore
(O3)
reduces to
xr=rx
, for all
r\in R
. This is simply the
classical univariate polynomial ring over
R
, and we write
R[x;\operatorname{id}_{R},0]=R[x]
. Moreover the formula (
54
) corresponds to usual multiplication of monomials. In this case, Theorem
3.12
becomes the classical Hilbert's Basis Theorem. Furthermore, we consider the
classical multivariate polynomial ring over
R
,
R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, as an iterated skew polynomial ring over
R
, where
\sigma_{i}=\operatorname{id}
and
\delta_{i}=0
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
.

Example 3.16

(Polynomial ring of endomorphism type)
.

Take
\delta=0
. Then
(O3)
becomes
xr=\sigma(r)x
, for all
r\in R
. In this case we write
R[x;\sigma,0]=R[x;\sigma]
. The formula (
54
) reduces to
(rx^{n})(sx^{m})=r\sigma^{n}(s)x^{n+m}
, for all
r,s\in R
and
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
. A widely studied, particular case is when
\sigma
is an automorphism of
R
.

Example 3.17

(Polynomial ring of derivation type)
.

Take
\sigma=\operatorname{id}_{R}
. Then
(O3)
becomes
xr=rx+\delta(r)
, for all
r\in R
. In this case we write
R[x;\operatorname{id}_{R},\delta]=R[x;\delta]
. Moreover, formula (
54
) simplifies to

(rx^{n})(sx^{m})=r\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\delta^{k}(s)x^{n+m-k},\qquad\mbox{for all }r,s\in R\mbox{ and }n,m\in\mathbb{N}.

The generalizations of Examples
3.16
and
3.17
to several variables (i.e., iterated skew polynomial rings) is straightforward and therefore omitted; a particular case of the later is discussed in Example
3.26
.

Example 3.18

(Ore algebras)
.

Since the setup in general iterated skew polynomial rings can be cumbersome, usually additional conditions may be imposed:

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(x_{j})=

\displaystyle\ x_{j},\qquad j<i,

(57)

\displaystyle\delta_{i}(x_{j})=

\displaystyle\ 0,\qquad j<i,

(58)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}=

\displaystyle\ \sigma_{j}\sigma_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n,

(59)

\displaystyle\delta_{i}\delta_{j}=

\displaystyle\ \delta_{j}\delta_{i},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n,

(60)

where the two last relations are understood to be restricted to
R
. Iterated skew polynomial rings satisfying these relations are common, but it does not seem to exist a standard name in the literature. In the case of one single variable (i.e., no iteration) the relations trivialize.

It can be shown that (
57
)-(
60
) are equivalent to the following relations:

\displaystyle x_{i}x_{j}=x_{j}x_{i},\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq n,

(61)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(R),\delta_{i}(R)\subseteq R,\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

(62)

Therefore, under these conditions the maps
\sigma_{i},\delta_{i}
can be seen as maps
\sigma_{i},\delta_{i}:R\rightarrow R
.

We mention a particular case of the above, distinguished by its well behavior on computational implementations (see e.g.
[
105
]
). Let
[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
be a classical multivariate polynomial ring over . If
A=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}][x_{1};\sigma_{n},\delta]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]
is an iterated Ore extension satisfying (
57
)-(
60
), then
A
is called an
Ore algebra
.

We present concrete cases of the above.

Example 3.19

(Enveloping universal algebra of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}()
)
.

Recall from Example
2.11
that a -basis for
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}()
is formed by

x=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}0&1\\
0&0\end{array}\right),\qquad y=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}0&0\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\qquad h=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),

and thus
U:=U(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}())
can be seen as a the -algebra generated by
x,y,h
subject to the relation
[x,y]=h
,
[h,x]=2x
and
[h,y]=-2y
. It is possible to show that
U
is isomorphic to either of the following iterated polynomial rings:

\displaystyle[x][h;\delta_{1}][y;\sigma_{2},\delta_{2}]\cong[h][x;\sigma_{1}][y;\sigma_{2},\delta_{2}],

where

\displaystyle\delta_{1}

\displaystyle=2x\frac{d}{dx},

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(h)

\displaystyle=h-2,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=x,

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(h)

\displaystyle=h+2,

\displaystyle\delta_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=-h,

\displaystyle\delta_{2}(h)

\displaystyle=0.

By Theorem
3.6
and Proposition
3.10
,
U
is a Noetherian domain.

Example 3.20

(Quantum enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}()
)
.

Recall from Example
2.13
that for
q\in
an invertible element such that
q\neq\pm 1
,
U_{q}:=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}())
is the -algebra generated by
e,f,k,k^{-1}
subject to the relations

\displaystyle kk^{-1}=k^{-1}k=1,

(63)

\displaystyle kek^{-1}=q^{2}e,

(64)

\displaystyle kfk^{-1}=q^{-2}f,

(65)

\displaystyle\left[e,f\right]=ef-fe=\frac{k-k^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}.

(66)

We realized that this algebra is in fact a Hopf algebra. In this example we will show that it can also be seen as an iterated skew polynomial ring.

Let
A_{0}:=[k,k^{-1}]
be the Laurent polynomial ring in the variable
k
, in which (
63
) is satisfied. Notice that
A_{0}
is a Noetherian domain and that
\{k^{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a -basis of
A_{0}
. Now, consider the automorphism
\sigma_{1}
of
A_{0}
given by
\sigma_{1}(k):=q^{2}k
and the respective Ore extension
A_{1}:=A_{0}[f;\sigma_{1}]
. Then, using a similar argument to the one given in the proof of Lemma
3.13
, we can prove that a -basis for
A_{1}
is
\{f^{j}k^{l}:j\in\mathbb{N},\,l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Moreover, by Theorem
3.12
,
A_{1}
is a Noetherian domain. We have
fk=\sigma_{1}(k)f=q^{2}kf
which corresponds to the relation (
65
). By the universal property of free algebras and Theorem
3.6
,
A_{1}
is isomorphic to the free algebra generated by
f,k,k^{-1}
subject to the relations (
63
) and (
65
).

Now we construct
A_{2}:=A_{1}[e;\sigma_{2},\delta]
. Let

\sigma_{2}(f^{j}k^{l}):=q^{-2l}f^{j}k^{l},\qquad j\in\mathbb{N},\,l\in\mathbb{Z}.

(67)

Then
\sigma_{2}
is an automorphism of
A_{1}
. If we denote by
\delta(f)(k)
the Laurent polynomial
\frac{k-k^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}
, let

\delta(k^{l}):=0,\qquad\delta(f^{j}k^{l}):=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}f^{j-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{l}.

(68)

We must verify that
\delta
is a
\sigma_{2}
-derivation of
A_{1}
. For that, it suffices to check that for every
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
l,n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\delta(f^{j}k^{l}f^{m}k^{n})=\sigma_{2}(f^{j}k^{l})\delta(f^{m}k^{n})+\delta(f^{j}k^{l})f^{m}k^{n}.

(69)

Indeed, starting from the right side of (
69
) and using (
65
), (
67
) and (
68
), we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(f^{j}k^{l})\delta(f^{m}k^{n})+\delta(f^{j}k^{l})f^{m}k^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}q^{-2l}f^{j}k^{l}f^{m-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}f^{j-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{l}f^{m}k^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}q^{-2l-2l(m-1)}f^{j+m-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{l+n}+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}q^{-2lm}f^{m+j-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i-2m}k)k^{l+n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}q^{-2lm}f^{m+j-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{l+n}+\sum_{i=m}^{j+m-1}q^{-2lm}f^{m+j-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{l+n}

\displaystyle=q^{-2lm}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{j+m-1}f^{j+m-1}\delta(f)(q^{-2i}k)k^{l+n}\right)=q^{-2lm}\delta(f^{j+m}k^{l+n})=\delta(f^{j}k^{l}f^{m}k^{n}).

Thus, in particular
\delta(f)=\frac{k-k^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}
and
\delta(k)=0
, whence
ek=\sigma_{2}(k)e+\delta(k)=q^{-2}ke
, which corresponds to (
64
), and
ef=\sigma_{2}(f)e+\delta(f)=fe+\frac{k-k^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}
, which is (
66
).

Therefore,
U_{q}
is isomorphic to
[k,k^{-1}][f;\sigma_{1}][e;\sigma_{2},\delta]
and hence, it is a Noetherian domain with -basis
\{e^{i}f^{j}k^{l}:i,j\in\mathbb{N},l\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

Example 3.21

(The algebra of shift operators)
.

Let
[t]
the classical univariate polynomial ring over a field . If
\sigma_{h}:[t]\rightarrow[t]
is the endomorphism defined by
\sigma_{h}(p(t))=p(t-h)
, with
p(t)\in[t]
, then the skew polynomial ring
S_{h}=[t][x_{h};\sigma_{h}]
over
[t]
is known as the
algebra of shift operators
. If
p(t),q(t)\in[t]
, the formula (
54
) becomes

p(t)x_{h}^{n}q(t)x_{h}^{m}=p(t)q(t-nh)x_{h}^{n+m},\qquad\mbox{for all }n,m\in\mathbb{N}.

Notice that
S_{h}
is an Ore algebra. When
=\mathbb{R}
and
h>0
, it is used to model time-delays systems
[
106
]
.

Example 3.22

(Weyl algebra)
.

Let
[t]
be as in Example
3.21
and denote by
\frac{d}{dt}
the derivate operator with respect to
t
. The skew polynomial ring
A_{1}()=[t][x;\frac{d}{dt}]
over
[t]
is known as the
first Weyl algebra
. If
p(t),q(t)\in[t]
the formula (
54
) becomes

p(t)x^{n}q(t)x^{m}=p(t)\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}q^{(k)}(t)x^{n+m-k},\qquad\mbox{for all }m,n\in\mathbb{N}.

Here,
q^{(k)}(t)
is the
k
-th derivate of
q(t)
with respect to
t
. The
n
-th Weyl algebra
(
n\geq 1
) is defined as the Ore algebra
A_{n}():=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}][x_{1};\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}]\cdots[x_{n};\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{n}}]
. One of the main applications of Weyl algebras is the theory of
D
-modules (see e.g.
[
107
]
).

Example 3.23

(The mixed algebra)
.

For every
h\in
, we define the
mixed algebra
(also known as the
algebra of delayed differential operators

[
106
]
) as
D_{h}:=[t][x;\frac{d}{dt}][x_{h};\sigma_{h}]
, where
\sigma_{h}
is as in Example
3.21
. We have
D_{h}=A_{1}()[x_{h};\delta_{h}]
and hence it is an Ore algebra.

Example 3.24

(The algebra for multidimensional discrete linear systems)
.

The Ore algebra defined as
D:=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}][x_{1};\sigma_{1}]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n}]
, where

\sigma_{i}(p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))=p(t_{1},\ldots,t_{i-1},t_{i}+1,t_{i+1},\ldots,t_{n}),\qquad\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq n,

is known as the
algebra for multidimensional discrete linear systems

[
106
]
.

More properties and examples of skew polynomial rings can be found in the literature (e.g.
[
104
,
9
]
).

3.2

PBW extensions

Although (iterated) skew polynomial rings describe a large amount of noncommutative algebras, these do not cover some remarkable examples, such as the generalized differential operator ring or the enveloping algebra of a finite dimensional Lie algebra. Hence, Bell and Goodearl defined a new family of rings that cover those having the PBW property and polynomial aspect
[
10
]
.

Definition 3.25

(PBW extension)
.

Let
R
and
A
be two rings. We say that
A
is a Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt (PBW) extension of
R
if the following conditions hold:

(PBW1)

A
contains
R
as a proper subring and
1_{R}=1_{A}
,

(PBW2)

(
PBW property
) There exist finitely many elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in A
such that
A
is a free left
R
-module with basis

\operatorname{Mon}(A):=\left\{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}:\alpha:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\right\},

(PBW3)

For each
r\in R
and every
1\leq i\leq n
,
x_{i}r-rx_{i}\in R
,

(PBW4)

For every
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
x_{i}x_{j}-x_{j}x_{i}\in R+Rx_{1}+\cdots+Rx_{n}
.

Under these conditions, we write
A=R\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
, and
R
will be called the
ring of coefficients
of the extension
A
.

The basis
\operatorname{Mon}(A)
is usually called
the set of standard monomials
(of
A
) and also denoted by
\operatorname{Mon}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Inspired by the PBW Theorem (see Example
2.10
),
\operatorname{Mon}(A)
is called a
PBW basis
for
A
. In general, the elements
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
do not commute when
i\neq j
. If only
(PBW1)
and
(PBW2)
hold, we say that
A
is a ring of left polynomial type over
R
with respect to
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
.

Before giving some properties, we review a few examples of PBW extensions adapted from
[
10
]
.

Example 3.26

(Ore extensions of derivation type)
.

Let
R
be a ring and let

A:=R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]

be an iterated skew polynomial ring of
R
satisfying (
57
)-(
60
) (or equivalently, (
61
)-(
62
)). We say that
A
is
an (iterated) Ore extension of derivation type
if
\sigma_{i}=\operatorname{id}_{R}
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
. These extensions are all PBW extension, since for every
r\in R
and
1\leq i,j\leq n
we have
x_{i}r-rx_{i}=\delta_{i}(r)
and
x_{i}x_{j}-x_{j}x_{i}=0
, proving
(PBW3)
and
(PBW4)
. Condition
(PBW1)
is trivial and
(PBW2)
is Lemma
3.13
. In particular, the classical multivariate polynomial ring (see Example
3.15
) and Weyl algebras (see Example
3.22
) are PBW extensions.

Nevertheless, not every (iterated) skew polynomial ring is a PBW extension. Indeed, by taking
A=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
with
\sigma\neq\operatorname{id}_{R}
, condition
(PBW3)
does not hold. A particular example of this is the algebra of shift operators (see Example
3.21
). The other inclusion is also not true, as the next example shows.

Example 3.27

(Universal enveloping algebra of a finite dimensional Lie algebra)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite dimensional -Lie algebra with ordered basis
X=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
. The PBW Theorem for the
U(\mathfrak{g})
(see Example
2.10
) guarantees that
(PBW1)
and
(PBW2)
are satisfied when we take
R=
. With this, it is immediate that
U(\mathfrak{g})
is a PBW extension of , since for all
k\in
and
x_{i},x_{j}\in X
,
x_{i}k-kx_{i}=0\in
, and
x_{i}x_{j}-x_{j}x_{i}=[x_{i},x_{j}]\in\mathfrak{g}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\subseteq+x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}
, which are precisely
(PBW3)
and
(PBW4)
. However, in general,
U(\mathfrak{g})
is not necessarily an iterated skew polynomial ring, since in the expansion of the product
x_{i}x_{j}
, the variables
x_{k}
(with
k>j
) may appear. Nonetheless, for some particular Lie algebras, the enveloping algebra can be seen both as PBW extension and as iterated skew polynomial ring (e.g. Example
3.19
).

We end this section by giving two additional examples of PBW extensions involving the algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
.

Example 3.28

(Tensor product with the universal enveloping algebra of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a -Lie algebra with basis
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i}
and let
R
be an arbitrary -algebra. The -algebra
R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})
is also a left
R
-module via the multiplication by elements of
R
.

If
W:=\{x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{t}}^{\alpha_{t}}:x_{i_{j}}\in X,\,\alpha_{i}\geq 0,\,t\geq 1\}
is the -basis for
U(\mathfrak{g})
given by the PBW Theorem, then
1\otimes W:=\{1\otimes z:z\in W\}
is an
R
-basis for
R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})
. Indeed, if
M
is a left
R
-module and
f:1\otimes W\rightarrow M
is any function, then we can induce a -bilinear map
\overline{f}:R\times U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow M
given by

\left(r,\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}\right)\mapsto r\cdot\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}f(1\otimes X_{i}),

with
X_{i}\in W
and
\lambda_{i}\in
. Hence, by the universal property of tensor products, we can uniquely induce a -linear map
f^{\prime}:R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow M
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\times U(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

is commutative, where
\iota
is the canonical map. In fact,
f^{\prime}
is a
R
-morphism since for every
s\in R
,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\left(s\cdot\left(r\otimes\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}\left(sr\otimes\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}\right)=\overline{f}\left(sr,\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=(sr)\cdot\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}f(1\otimes X_{i})=s\cdot\left(r\cdot\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}f(1\otimes X_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=s\cdot\overline{f}\left(r,\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}\right)=s\cdot f^{\prime}\left(r\otimes\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X_{i}\right).

Moreover, it is clear that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

is commutative, where
j
is the inclusion map. Additionally, by the uniqueness of
\overline{f}
, the map
f^{\prime}
is the only one satisfying such commutativity. Hence, since every function from
1\otimes W
to an arbitrary left module of
R
can be extended to a
R
-morphism from
R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})
to such module,
1\otimes W
is indeed an
R
-basis.

Notice that
R\hookrightarrow R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})
via
r\mapsto r\otimes 1=r\cdot(1\otimes 1)
, which corresponds to
(PBW1)
. If
\mathfrak{g}
is finite-dimensional with
X=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, then we just proved that

1\otimes W=\{(1\otimes x_{1})^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots(1\otimes x_{n})^{\alpha_{n}}:\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\}=\operatorname{Mon}(1\otimes x_{1},\ldots,1\otimes x_{n})

is an
R
-basis for
R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})
, which is
(PBW2)
. Furthermore,
(PBW3)
and
(PBW4)
hold, for if
r\in R
and
1\leq i,j\leq n
, then

(r\otimes 1)(1\otimes x_{i})-(1\otimes x_{i})(r\otimes 1)=r\otimes x_{i}-r\otimes x_{i}=0\in R,

\displaystyle(1\otimes x_{i})(1\otimes x_{j})-(1\otimes x_{j})(1\otimes x_{i})

\displaystyle=1\otimes x_{i}x_{j}-x_{j}x_{i}=1\otimes[x_{i},x_{j}]

\displaystyle\in R+R(1\otimes x_{1})+\cdots+R(1\otimes x_{n}).

Thus
R\otimes U(\mathfrak{g})
is a PBW extension of
R
.

Example
3.28
holds if we change
U(\mathfrak{g})
to any PBW extension with a finite number of indeterminates.

Example 3.29

(Crossed product with the universal enveloping algebra of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a -Lie algebra with basis
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i}
and let
R
be an arbitrary -algebra. We say that a -algebra
S
is a
crossed product
of
R
by
U(\mathfrak{g})
if the following conditions hold:

(i)

S
contains
R
as a proper subalgebra,

(ii)

There exists an injective -algebra morphism
\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow S
, denoted by
x\mapsto\overline{x}
,

(iii)

\overline{x}r-r\overline{x}\in R
and
r\mapsto\overline{x}r-r\overline{x}
is a -derivation of
R
, for all
r\in R
,

(iv)

\overline{x}\overline{y}-\overline{y}\overline{x}\in\overline{[x,y]}+R
, for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
,

(v)

S
is a free right left
R
-module with the standard monomials over
\{\overline{x_{i}}\}
as a basis.

In such case, we write
S=R*U(\mathfrak{g})
. By definition if
X
is finite (that is,
\mathfrak{g}
is finite-dimensional), then
R*U(\mathfrak{g})
is a PBW extension of
R
. Particular examples of crossed products with the universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra can be found in
[
9
, 1.7.13]
.

3.3

Skew PBW extensions

We saw in the previous section that if a skew polynomial ring
A=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
is such that
\sigma\neq\operatorname{id}_{R}
, then
A
is not a PBW extension of
R
. In order to solve this incompatibility without losing the polynomial behavior, Gallego and Lezama introduced
skew PBW extensions
as a generalization of PBW extensions
[
12
]
and Ore extensions
[
7
]
. Since then, several authors have studied algebraic and geometrical properties of these objects
[
108
,
12
,
113
,
114
,
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
,
119
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
112
]
. As a matter of fact, a book containing research results about these extensions has been recently published (see
[
11
]
).

Definition 3.30

(Skew PBW extension)
.

Let
R
and
A
be two rings. We say that
A
is a
skew PBW extension of
R
(also called
\sigma
-PBW extension) if the following conditions hold:

(SPBW1)

A
contains
R
as a proper subring and
1_{R}=1_{A}
,

(SPBW2)

There exist finitely many elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in A
such that
A
is a free left
R
-module with basis

\operatorname{Mon}(A):=\operatorname{Mon}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\left\{x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}:\alpha:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\right\},

(SPBW3)

For each
r\in R-\{0\}
and every
1\leq i\leq n
, there exists
c_{i,r}\in R-\{0\}
such that
x_{i}r-c_{i,r}x_{i}\in R
,

(SPBW4)

For every
1\leq i,j\leq n
, there exists
c_{i,j}\in R-\{0\}
such that we have the relationship
x_{i}x_{j}-c_{i,j}x_{j}x_{i}\in R+Rx_{1}+\cdots+Rx_{n}
.

Under these conditions we write
A=\sigma(R)\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
, and
R
is called the
ring of coefficients
of the extension.

Remark 3.31
.

Several facts can be immediately deduced from Definition
3.30
.

(i)

By
(SPBW2)
, the elements
c_{i,r}
and
c_{i,j}
of
(SPBW3)
and
(SPBW4)
are unique.

(ii)

For
i=j
, in
(SPBW4)
,
c_{i,i}=1
. Indeed, since
x_{i}^{2}-c_{i,i}x_{i}^{2}=0
, then
1-c_{i,i}=0
. If
r=0
, we define
c_{i,0}=0
.

(iii)

Every
c_{i,j}\in R
, with
1\leq i<j\leq n
, is left invertible. Indeed,
c_{i,j}
and
c_{j,i}
are such that

\displaystyle x_{i}x_{j}-c_{i,j}x_{j}x_{i}\in R+Rx_{1}+\cdots+Rx_{n},

\displaystyle x_{j}x_{i}-c_{j,i}x_{i}x_{j}\in R+Rx_{1}+\cdots+Rx_{n}.

Since
\operatorname{Mon}(A)
is an
R
-basis then
1=c_{i,j}c_{j,i}
.

(iv)

We denote the elements of
\operatorname{Mon}(A)
as
x^{\alpha}
when it is important to highlight the exponents
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
. An alternative notation for an arbitrary element of
\operatorname{Mon}(A)
is using the capital letter
X
. By
(SPBW2)
, each element
f\in A-\{0\}
has a unique representation in the form
f=c_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+c_{t}X_{t}
, with
c_{i}\in R-\{0\}
and
X_{i}\in\operatorname{Mon}(A)
, for every
1\leq i\leq t
.

(v)

It is clear that the verification of
(SPBW2)
in most cases can be cumbersome. There are several techniques for that purpose, including Lemma
3.13
for skew polynomial rings, computation of Gröbner bases of two-sided ideals for free algebras
[
11
]
, the Bergman's Diamond Lemma
[
120
]
and the existence theorem for skew PBW extensions
[
121
]
.

The following result justifies the notation for skew PBW extensions.

Proposition 3.32

(
[
12
, Proposition 3]
)
.

Let
A
be a skew PBW extension of
R
. Then for each
1\leq i\leq n
there exist an injective ring endomorphism
\sigma_{i}:R\rightarrow R
and a
\sigma_{i}
-derivation
\delta_{i}:R\rightarrow R
such that
x_{i}r=\sigma_{i}(r)x_{i}+\delta_{i}(r)
, for every
r\in R
.

Proof 3.33
.

By
(SPBW3)
, for each
1\leq i\leq n
and every
r\in R
, there exist elements
c_{i,r},r_{i}\in R
such that
x_{i}r=c_{i,r}x_{i}+r_{i}
. Since
\operatorname{Mon}(A)
is a
R
-basis of
A
, these elements are unique for
r
, so we can define the maps
\sigma_{i},\delta_{i}:R\rightarrow R
by
\sigma_{i}(r):=c_{i,r}
and
\delta_{i}(r):=r_{i}
. Moreover, it is clear that if
r\neq 0
, then
c_{i,r}\neq 0
, which means that
\sigma_{i}
is injective. It is easy to check that
\sigma_{i}
is an endomorphism and that
\delta_{i}
is a
\sigma_{i}
-derivation.

We mention some particular cases.

Definition 3.34

(Quasi-commutative skew PBW extension, bijective skew PBW extension)
.

Let
A
be a skew PBW extension of
R
.

(i)

A is said to be
quasi-commutative
if
(SPBW3)
and
(SPBW4)
are replaced by

(SPBW3’)

For every
1\leq i\leq n
and
r\in R-\{0\}
, there exists
c_{i,r}\in R-\{0\}
such that
x_{i}r=c_{i,r}x_{i}
.

(SPBW4’)

For every
1\leq i,j\leq n
, there exists
c_{i,j}\in R-\{0\}
such that
x_{j}x_{i}=c_{i,j}x_{i}x_{j}
.

(ii)

A
is said to be
bijective
if
\sigma_{i}
is bijective, for every
1\leq i\leq n
, and each
c_{i,j}
is invertible, for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Remark 3.35
.

If
A
is a quasi-commutative skew PBW extension of
R
, then
A
is isomorphic to an iterated skew polynomial ring of endomorphism type
[
119
, Theorem 2.3]
. Nevertheless, skew PBW extensions of endomorphism type (i.e., with all derivations
\delta_{i}
zero) are indeed more general than iterated skew polynomial rings of the same type. To clearly illustrate this, we consider the situations of only two and three indeterminates.

For a skew polynomial ring
R[x;\sigma_{x}][y;\sigma_{y}]
of endomorphism type we have the relations

xr=\sigma_{x}(r)x,\quad yr=\sigma_{y}(r)y,\quad yx=\sigma_{y}(x)y,

for any
r\in R
. On the other hand, for a skew PBW extension
\sigma(R)\langle x,y\rangle
of endomorphism type we deduce from Definition
3.30
the equations

xr=\sigma_{1}(r)x,\quad yr=\sigma_{2}(r)y,\quad yx=d_{1,2}xy+r_{0}+r_{1}x+r_{2}y,

for some elements
d_{1,2},r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}\in R
. When we compare the defining relations of both algebraic structures, it is clear that the former is more general.

Similarly, for an iterated skew polynomial ring
R[x;\sigma_{x}][y;\sigma_{y}][z;\sigma_{z}]
of endomorphism type we have

\displaystyle xr=\sigma_{x}(r)x,\quad yr=\sigma_{y}(r)y,\quad zr=\sigma_{z}(r)z,

\displaystyle yx=\sigma_{y}(x)y,\quad zx=\sigma_{z}(x)z,\quad zy=\sigma_{z}(y)z

for any
r\in R
. On the other hand, for a skew PBW extension
\sigma(R)\langle x,y,z\rangle
of endomorphism type we deduce

\displaystyle xr=\sigma_{1}(r)x,\quad yr=\sigma_{2}(r)y,\quad zr=\sigma_{3}(r)z,

\displaystyle yx=d_{1,2}xy+r_{0}+r_{1}x+r_{2}y+r_{3}z,\quad zx=d_{1,3}xz+r_{0}^{\prime}+r_{1}^{\prime}x+r_{2}^{\prime}y+r_{3}^{\prime}z

\displaystyle zy=d_{2,3}yz+r_{0}^{\prime\prime}+r_{1}^{\prime\prime}x+r_{2}^{\prime\prime}y+r_{3}^{\prime\prime}z,

for some elements
d_{1,2},d_{1,3},d_{2,3},r_{0},r_{0}^{\prime},r_{0}^{\prime\prime},r_{1},r_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime\prime},r_{2},r_{2}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime\prime},r_{3},r_{3}^{\prime},r_{3}^{\prime\prime}\in R
. As we can see, as the number of indeterminates increases the generality of skew PBW extensions of endomorphism type becomes more notorious.

We mention two remarkable properties of skew PBW extensions that are similar to those of classical polynomial rings and skew polynomial rings.

By
(SPBW4)
, for every
1\leq i,j\leq n
, we know that there exist a unique finite set of constants
c_{i,j},d_{i,j},a_{ij}^{k}\in R-\{0\}
such that
x_{i}x_{j}=c_{i,j}x_{j}x_{i}+a^{(1)}_{ij}x_{1}+\cdots+a^{(n)}_{ij}x_{n}+d_{ij}
. Such constants, together with the coefficient ring
R
, the number of variables
n
, the injective endomorphism
\sigma_{k}
and the
\sigma_{k}
-derivations
\delta_{k}
are known as the
parameters
of the extension.

Theorem 3.36

(Universal property of skew PBW extensions,
[
121
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
A=\sigma(R)\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
be a skew PBW extension of
R
with corresponding parameters
R
,
n
,
\sigma_{k}
,
\delta_{k}
,
c_{ij}
,
d_{ij}
,
a_{ij}^{(k)}
, for
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq n
. Let
B
a ring together with a ring morphism
\phi:R\rightarrow B
and elements
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
such that:

(i)

y_{k}\phi(r)=\phi(\sigma_{k}(r))y_{k}+\phi(\delta_{k}(r))
, for every
r\in R
and
1\leq k\leq n
,

(ii)

y_{j}y_{i}=\phi(c_{ij})y_{i}y_{j}+\phi(a_{ij}^{(1)})y_{1}+\cdots+\phi(a_{ij}^{n})y_{n}+\phi(d_{ij})
, for every
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Then, there exists a unique ring morphism
\psi:A\rightarrow B
such that
\psi(x_{i})=y_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, and the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(R)\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

(70)

is commutative, where
\iota
is the inclusion map.

Theorem 3.37

(Hilbert's Basis Theorem for skew PBW extensions,
[
119
, Corollary 2.4]
)
.

Let
A=\sigma(R)\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
be a bijective skew PBW extension of
R
. If
R
is a left (resp. right) Noetherian ring then
A
is also a left (resp. right) Noetherian ring.

The proof of this last result uses techniques of graduation-filtration, since the graded associated ring of
A
is an iterated skew polynomial ring of endomorphism type (see e.g.
[
11
, Section 3.1]
).

We end this section by mentioning some examples of skew PBW extensions, which were adapted from
[
11
,
12
,
119
]
.

Example 3.38

(PBW extensions)
.

Any PBW extension is a bijective skew PBW extension since in that case
\sigma_{i}=\operatorname{id}_{R}
(
1\leq i\leq n
) and
c_{i,j}=1
(
1\leq i,j\leq n
).

Example 3.39

(Skew polynomial rings of injective type)
.

Any skew polynomial ring
A=R[x;\sigma,\delta]
with
\sigma
injective is a skew PBW extension,
R[x;\sigma,\delta]=\sigma(R)\langle x\rangle
. If additionally
\delta=0
, then
R[x;\sigma]
is quasi-commutative.

Moreover, an iterated skew polynomial ring
A=R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]
is a skew PBW extension of
R
if the following conditions hold:

(i)

\sigma_{i}
is injective, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

(ii)

\sigma_{i}(R),\delta_{i}(R)\subseteq R
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

(iii)

There exist
c_{i},d_{i}\in R
such that
c_{i}
is left invertible and
\sigma_{j}(x_{i})=c_{i}x_{i}+d_{i}
, for
i<j
.

(iv)

\delta_{j}(x_{i})\in R+Rx_{1}+\cdots+Rx_{n}
, for
i<j
.

Under these conditions, we have
A=R[x_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]=\sigma(R)\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle
and
A
is called
of injective type
.

A particular case of such situation is given by iterated skew polynomial rings satisfying (
57
)-(
60
) with each
\sigma_{i}
being injective. If specifically
R=[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
, then we have an Ore algebra (cf. Example
3.18
), and

[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}][x_{1};\sigma_{n},\delta]\cdots[x_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]=\sigma([t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}])\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle.

Hence, concrete examples are the algebra of shift operators
S_{h}
(Example
3.21
), the Weyl algebras
A_{n}()
(Example
3.22
), the mixed algebra
D_{h}
( Example
3.23
) and the algebra for multidimensional discrete linear systems
D
(Example
3.24
). Observe that all of these examples are not PBW extensions.

Example 3.40

(Additive analogue of the Weyl algebra)
.

Given elements
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\in-\{0\}
, let
A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
be the algebra generated by
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
together with the relations

\displaystyle x_{j}x_{i}=x_{i}x_{j},\qquad y_{j}y_{i}=y_{i}y_{j},\qquad\mbox{for }1\leq i,j\leq n,

\displaystyle y_{i}x_{j}=x_{j}y_{i},\qquad\mbox{for }i\neq j,

\displaystyle y_{i}x_{i}=q_{i}x_{i}y_{i}+1,\qquad\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq n.

A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
is known as the
additive analogue of the Weyl algebra

[
122
]
and it is isomorphic to
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}][y_{1};\sigma_{1},\delta_{1}]\cdots[y_{n};\sigma_{n},\delta_{n}]
over
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, where

\displaystyle\sigma_{j}(y_{i})=y_{i},\qquad\delta_{j}(y_{i})=0,\qquad\mbox{for }1\leq i<j\leq n,

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(x_{j})=x_{j},\qquad\delta_{i}(x_{j})=0,\qquad\mbox{for }i\neq j,

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(x_{i})=q_{i}x_{i},\qquad\delta_{i}(x_{i})=1,\qquad\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq n.

Since
A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
is an iterated Ore extension of injective type, it is also a skew PBW extension of
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. Moreover, it is bijective and

A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})=\sigma([x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])\langle y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rangle.

Nonetheless, notice that
A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
can also be viewed as a skew PBW extension of , by putting
A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})=\sigma()\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\rangle
. If
q_{i}=q\neq 0
, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, then
A_{n}(q_{1},\ldots,q_{n})
becomes the
algebra of
q
-differential operators

[
123
]
.

Example 3.41

(Multiplicative analogue of the Weyl algebra)
.

Given
\lambda_{ij}\in-\{0\}
, with
